## Global Data Watch

• Expansion resilience reinforces caution on disinflation progress

Easing cycles for Fed, ECB, BoE all to start around mid-year

• China’s growth hits target but imbalances remain key concern

● Next week: DM flash Jan PMIs (US down, EMU up), US 4Q GDP (2%)

## Shades of grey

It is often said that economic forecasts lend credibility to weather predictions. To be sure, both disciplines deal with complex systems in which unprecedented recent developments (COVID and climate change) have increased uncertainty. While we continue to emphasize limited visibility in key elements of the 2024 outlook— most notably whether we are moving on to a path of a sustainable expansion—it is notable that markets and economic forecasters are increasingly embracing a softlanding scenario.

On growth, we see low 1H24 recession risks. With financial conditions having eased materially and moderating inflation boosting household purchasing power, pressures that could spark a near-term break have abated. The year-end signal from the global composite PMI and labor markets aligns with our forecast that growth is proceeding at a trend-like pace as we turn into the new year.

Sustaining growth at this pace will likely require a narrowing in last year<sup>’</sup>s wide sectoral and regional gaps. The latest signals around convergence are not encouraging. A 1.3%ar rise 2H23 global factory output was more muted than expected and the recovery continues to stand on a fragile base as output gains are heavily concentrated in Asia tech activity (Figure 1). DM final goods demand was weak in 2H23, held back by Western Europe, which led to a pickup in inventory accumulation. The message from survey data is weak, with the December global manufacturing PMI sliding back into contractionary territory and this week<sup>’</sup>s US January regional Fed surveys nosediving. We expect next week<sup>’</sup>s DM January flash PMI to move even lower.

Recent signs of life in global goods demand is a bright spot. Global consumer goods spending growth has perked back up to a solid 2.4%ar in the three months through November, and this week<sup>’</sup>s US retail sales report for December was strong. Our global capex nowcaster has also picked up in recent months. However, any sustained pickup in global goods demand will likely require better news from Western Europe, where a cautious consumer has been a sustained growth drag. A sharp slide in European inflation provides the fuel for lift as real labor income is now accelerating at a faster than 2%ar. But the clock is ticking. The loss of pricing power for businesses will, absent a pickup in domestic spending, eventually motivate a pullback in hiring.

After two years of 5%ar gains, global core inflation tumbled to a 3.3%ar in 2H23. While optimism is increasing that the “last mile<sup>”</sup> of disinflation will soon follow, there are reasons to be cautious. Most of last year<sup>’</sup>s disinflation came in goods but commodity prices are no longer falling and traded goods prices point to a moderating deflationary impulse from China (Figure 2). The recent disruptions in Red Sea trade could add as much as 0.7%-pt to annualized global core goods inflation in 1H24. Positive inflation outcomes look likely to require material core service disinflation. But absent a material easing in labor markets or weak service sector demand, fundamental supports for this slide will not be in place. On balance, we maintain global core inflation of 3%ar in 1H24.

Against this backdrop, central bankers have become more optimistic that they can calibrate policy stances that are perceived to be materially restrictive. However, this optimism is cautious and the nearly unanimous message from a large number of DM central banks in recent weeks, including the Fed and ECB, is that rate cuts are unlikely to start this quarter (Figure 3). The realization of our forecast for growth resilience and sticky core inflation aligns with a more gradual midyear start to DM easing.

Figure 3: Policy rate expectations, end-2024
![](images/347c727a6d591a18f45b9a20e9b142e20b5d91ff54ad3bd0d7bd7b5368d62c6c.jpg)

Ever since Chair Powell<sup>’</sup>s dovish press conference in December, Fed rhetoric has attempted to temper market enthusiasm for March cuts—including comments this week from the more dovish Atlanta Fed president Bostic that he expected the first cut not to come until 3Q24. In the Euro area, a weak growth backdrop and sharper drop in inflation warrant an earlier ease. Even still, President Lagarde indicated a “summer<sup>”</sup> start to rate cuts and pushed back forcefully against market expectations for as much as 150bp of cuts this year. Recognizing that incoming reports have shifted the balance of risks, we have pulled forward the start of ECB rate cuts to June, in line with our forecast for the Fed. We also now look for BoE easing to start in August. Next week we expect Norges Bank to remain on hold following its surprise hike in December.

## BoJ to tighten guidance, not tools

Being the only central bank to have yet raised its policy rate from the emergency lows of the pandemic, the BoJ is charting its own course and remains focused on assuring the rise in inflation over the past two years is sustainable. Despite the weak headline figures in Japan<sup>’</sup>s latest CPI inflation (December) and wage growth (November) reports, the underlying details point to ongoing firming of inflation dynamics. We think the recent data, alongside firms<sup>’</sup> indications to raise wages, are helping to gradually bolster the BoJ<sup>’</sup>s confidence that such structural changes are indeed taking place—a necessary condition to start normalizing policy. That, in turn, should be enough for the BoJ to change the tone of its policy statement next week, removing the strong easing bias from its forward guidance and potentially introducing more flexibility into its JGB purchases. But greater confidence will be needed to remove its negative interest rate policy, which we continue to expect to be a mid-year event.

## BoC still battling sticky core inflation

The Bank of Canada is expected to remain on hold next week and, as with most other DM central banks, recent comments have pushed back against an early policy pivot. Indeed, stubbornly elevated BoC core inflation measures—stuck in a 3% to 4% range for the past year—are challenging a return to the 2% target. The latest 4Q Business Outlook Survey suggests a gradual normalization of expected inflation, wage growth, and corporate pricing behavior. The BoC is in no hurry to ease, and it is hard to see a softening of the currently hawkish forward guidance next week. This contrast in inflation outlooks has led markets to shift toward lower odds of the BoC easing than the Fed during 1H24 and a shallower BoC cutting cycle this year. We still expect the first BoC cut in 2Q23, as signs of an expected downward trend in inflation and wage growth, along with weaker demand, should become apparent by then.

## Imbalances a later risk to China growth

China<sup>’</sup>s 4Q23 GDP growth came in broadly as expected at 5.8%q/q, saar, resulting in full-year growth (5.2%y/y) modestly above the official target of “around 5%.<sup>”</sup> But the latest data also point to persistent imbalances in the economy as policy favors production and investment over consumption. December retail sales disappointed (declining 0.2%m/m) while various already-depressed measures of housing activity fell further. Soft home sales look to have spread from private to SOE developers, and home price declines expanded to toptier cities. Manufacturing output also fell in December (-0.8%m/m) but earlier strong gains means 4Q IP is running 10.6%ar above 3Q. FAI in manufacturing and infrastructure moved up as well thanks to policy supports.

Recent policy actions in China should allow growth to remain around 5%ar during 1H24, and we continue to think largescale stimulus is unlikely. This week<sup>’</sup>s hold by the PBOC (against market expectations for a rate cut) was in line with our expectations. Reports of a ¥1tn special government bond look to be a change in the composition of the budget deficit (3.8% of GDP) rather than additional fiscal support. But sluggish domestic demand is feeding deflationary pressures, and the GDP deflator fell 1.4%oya in 4Q (Figure 4). This pressure could last longer should the policy-induced imbalance between supply and demand persist. Our forecast for 2H24 growth to moderate to a 4.3%ar pace could face downside risks if deflation weighs further on consumption or the housing market correction spreads.

![](images/914f81eaae21a9e7a7a50c459a44a086cc0f2eff66642914009040641f810780.jpg)

## EM Asia CBs ponder softening inflation

Signs of softening in core services inflation are opening the door for policy easing among some EM Asia central banks, well after rate-cutting cycles began elsewhere in EM. Recent data point to cooling labor markets in Korea and Hong Kong; weak travel flows from Mainland China also likely weighed on tourism-related sectors. These developments support our expectation for sequential easing of core inflation across the region during 1H24, and we see some risk that Indonesia and possibly Thailand could ease earlier than forecast. But financial conditions could accelerate policy easing: Bank Indonesia held policy steady this week but has been focused on external stability concerns. Further easing of global financial conditions would prompt us to bring forward our forecast for two 25bp rate cuts by BI in 2H24. These cuts are likely to be preceded by a normalization of liquidity conditions.

## Upside-down growth surprises in LatAm

For much of last year, Mexico<sup>’</sup>s economic growth outperformed the rest of Latin America. While the divergence likely continued in 4Q23, recent data signal a shift in risks for this year<sup>’</sup>s outlook: stronger-than-expected in Brazil and the Andeans, and weaker in Mexico. For Brazil, we anticipate some payback from weakened agriculture in 2H23 driving a substantial acceleration in growth this year. Meanwhile, we revised up Colombia<sup>’</sup>s GDP forecast to 1.2% for 2024, and data confirm an expected firming of growth in Chile and Peru. A more tempered El Niño could lead to subdued inflation as well in the region, especially Peru. By contrast, while we continue to see above-trend growth in Mexico for the first half of this year, we recently revised down our 4Q23 GDP forecast to 2.5%ar on cautionary signals from confidence, sales, and labor market indicators.

## Some hawkish news for some EMEA CBs

A range of recent developments are likely to lead several EMEA central banks to retain their hawkish guidance against the broader trend toward easing expected elsewhere in EM. In South Africa, markets see elevated chances of a March move, but we think the easing cycle kicks off only in May. The departure of one dovish-leaning MPC member reinforces that view, while the governor suggested the SARB needs to see inflation come down to 4.5% (from 5.5%oya in November) to start easing. Recent news on El Niño (solid rains) and fiscal policy (benign despite an election year) have helped reduce inflation risks. By contrast, in Israel the fiscal deficit deteriorated more than expected, to 6.6% of GDP, largely due to increased defense spending. This sizable impulse comes on top of a recovery in consumer demand as the economy adjusts to the wartime reality. This confluence should constrain the BoI<sup>’</sup>s space to ease later this year, even with very soft inflation recently. We look for another 25bp cut in February.

In Türkiye, we expect the CBRT to hike by 250bp to 45% next week as it signaled for tightening to be completed as soon as possible. The inflation outlook deteriorated in January due to a higher-than-expected minimum wage hike, automatic tax hikes, and administered price hikes. Hawkish forward guidance should leave the door open for further CBRT rate hikes if needed. We maintain our end-2024 policy rate forecast at 45% to rein in inflation, but do not rule out further hikes beyond January. We also do not expect premature rate cuts this year.

## Nowcast of global growth: Top-down downside risk

The J.P. Morgan forecast for 1Q24 global real GDP was unchanged this week at 2.3%ar (Figure 1). An unexpected 0.6% jump in last month<sup>’</sup>s US retail sales data—echoing improved trend growth in our November global goods spending aggregate—suggests solid momentum in consumer spending through year-end, supported by wage gains and fading inflation. Our capex nowcaster also suggests a modest pickup in 4Q business equipment spending. At the same time, incoming data suggest a more modest global manufacturing sector recovery than previously anticipated, with factory output in the US and China slowing into year-end. We think gains in consumer spending amidst resilient labor markets will continue to support a GDP expansion through 1H24. However, whether final goods demand provides the lift necessary to promote a wider recovery in industry remains to be seen.

Figure 1: J.P. Morgan global GDP % change saar; forecast by date made
![](images/298a2f2f0224dc9930cbff94d84b7a252108b2a4e33da713306ed849670effa1.jpg)

The top-down global nowcaster moved sideways this week at 1.6%ar, continuing to suggest 0.7%-pt of downside risk to our forecast (Figure 2). The bottom-up nowcaster, meanwhile, fell 0.4%-pt and is now just 0.3%-pt above our forecast for the same country set. The nowcaster suggests upside risks to growth in China (+1.3%-pts) and slight downside risks in the Euro area (-0.2%-pt), but balanced risks in the US. The nowcasters are less accurate early in the quarter, using model estimates to fill out much of the quarter<sup>’</sup>s missing data.

In this week<sup>’</sup>s Daily Economic Briefings, we updated our global aggregates for factory output and consumer goods spending through November. Though IP rose 0.3% in November, this was largely due to a 1.3% gain in China. More broadly, manufacturing continues to look lackluster at best, with the modest recovery in 2H23 concentrated in Asia<sup>’</sup>s tech sector. Going forward, the path of output will largely hinge on final demand. On that front, news has been encouraging; our global real consumer goods aggregate rose 0.3% in November for a third consecutive gain, and early December data suggest a further 0.9% move up in the US. Our more timely global auto sales aggregate also strengthened 1.3% in December, though this rise was narrowly based in the US and China.

## J.P. Morgan Market Watch

## Bonds

Bond yields rose this week following hawkish central bank communication, strong data, some concerns that shipping disruptions could reignite supply chain woes and some mean reversion of the Oct-Dec rally. In the US, it now looks like real consumption rose about 0.3% in December and was up around 2.5% saar in 4Q23. With yields back at their highest levels since early December, TIPS breakevens somewhat rich in our framework, and positioning more neutral, we are turning more constructive on duration. Despite the aggressive bear steepening recently, most long-end pairs remain too flat relative to the market<sup>’</sup>s Fed policy and inflation expectations, and the Fed<sup>’</sup>s share of the Treasury market, though this mispricing is much smaller than a month ago. We maintain 5s/30s steepeners and 2s/5s flatteners.

In the Euro Area, we think the ECB will almost certainly remain on hold next week. Recent comments have pushed back against early rate cut expectations (March, April), but have left June open for a first cut. We now pull forward the first cut to June and we assume that the ECB skips July and then starts back-to-back cuts in September. This implies 100bp of cuts in 2024 (vs the 75bp we had before) and that rates still reach 2% around mid-2025. We keep a strategic long in 5Y Germany and stay tactically bullish on 1Yx1Y €STR. We maintain a constructive medium-term bias on intra-EMU and €-SSA spreads.

In EM local markets, we are OW duration overall with OWs in Latam (OWs in Brazil, Colombia, Uruguay, Peru) and EMEA EM (OWs in Czechia, Poland, South Africa vs UW in Romania) against a MW stance in Asia (OW Indo vs UW Thai GBs) (EMOS, Jan 11<sup>th</sup>).

## Credit

We feel it<sup>’</sup>s time to be more negative in US HG. Spreads at 110bp are just 2bp wide to the post-GFC tightest level. It may not be a floor for spreads, but it does highlight just how much good news is priced into the market currently, and there are many issues that could cause spreads to widen. When spreads are as tight as they are now, they are susceptible to any lessthan-good news. Our YE spread forecast is 125bp, so we are not bearish on spreads from a longer-term perspective—we expect them to remain below the long-term average this year. Demand is clearly overwhelming supply in HG, especially at the long end. As a result, HG bond spreads hit new lows of 110bp this week, the tightest since November 2021. This has come on the heels of yields rising by \~20bp YTD, which has kept the yield buyer well engaged at the long end while money managers are raising cash in the belly to fund new issue

purchases. January supply MTD is already at \$136bn, but clearly this isn<sup>’</sup>t enough to satisfy surging demand to start the year. The vast majority of this demand is driven by yields (Credit Market Outlook & Strategy, Jan 19<sup>th</sup>).

Spreads in mortgage credit are likely to stay range bound in the near-term, but the seemingly inevitable decline of longer-term yields in 2H24 means it could be the best time to load up on fixed-rate bonds. Yields have likely peaked but spreads, priced to perfection, could leak wider (RMBS Credit Commentary, Jan 19<sup>th</sup>).

Figure 1: Year-to-date returns %
![](images/619027d3566aa29dc90c144284c635fbca91948ca0e5194b405b8087f44ab885.jpg)
Source: J.P. Morgan Global Economics

In Euro Credit, this week saw the second strongest inflow into retail funds on record at €2.9bn. While we are hesitant to fight the technicals, we do see signs of complacency in the market. In particular, we continue to think that shipping disruptions are being underpriced. We remain cautious, although we generally prefer positioning for weakness through synthetic hedges rather than selling credit risk in cash bonds, except for where relative value has become so compressed that investors can move up in quality without giving up much carry (European Credit Weekly, Jan 19<sup>th</sup>).

## Currencies

Disinflation optimism in rates and the dollar has started to be tested by new year dataflow. Even after this week<sup>’</sup>s repricing, the USD curve remains aggressively priced for rate cuts, while other DM curves have room to catch down to the

US. The recent widening in inflation breakevens also bears close watching for signs of payback for the outsized financial conditions loosening of 4Q23. Both suggest continued dollar firmness from both sides of US - RoW rate differentials, even as the greenback<sup>’</sup>s late December undershoot has now corrected.

On macro trade recommendations, we maintain exposure to strategic trading themes including value/carry rotation, cyclical sequencing, EUR-funding and lingering US exceptionalism. Short EUR/JPY and EUR/USD trades in options expired OTM. Long SEK vs CZK & EUR, long USD/CAD, long AUD vs NZD & CAD, long JPY vs USD. We stay OW EM FX, favouring the high carry basket and recovery trades (FXMW, Jan 19<sup>th</sup>).

## Commodities

The record warm December shifts into frigid January impacting both supply and demand for energy. We adjust our US crude and condensate production forecast down by 365 kbd for January and cut natural gas supply by 3.6 Bcf/ day. An unseasonably warm December has likely lowered global oil demand by over 700 kbd and lower-48 US states natural gas demand by nearly 8Bcf/day during the last month of the year. We anticipate that freeze-offs could impact January natgas production by as much as 3.6 Bcf/day. Meanwhile in agriculture, the recent cold spell in the US poses potential risk to the 2024 winter wheat crop (Global Commodities, Jan 18<sup>th</sup>).

What is a balanced market worth? Our view remains that oil demand growth will slow from a very strong 1.8 mbd in 2023 to a still strong 1.5 mbd in 2024, underpinned by a moderating but still resilient global economic expansion and remaining pent-up oil demand. In 2024, we see gains in non-OPEC supply sufficient to cover global demand growth, with Brent oil price averaging \$83. Yet, in most recent examples of balanced markets in 2016 and 2019, Brent averaged \$45 and \$64, respectively. Global oil liquids inventories today are substantially below pre-COVID five-year averages. New equilibrium of lower oil inventories and their larger weight in the oil pricing model suggest that even in a regime of a strong US shale production growth, in a balanced market, Brent oil is worth more today than in the past. Oil impact of shipping disruptions in the Red Sea is likely to be small and easily handled (Oil Markets Weekly, Jan 19<sup>th</sup>).

Copper treatment and refining charges (TC/RCs) lurch towards a breaking point as they continued to nosedive this week, continuing a sharp fall that kicked off in 4Q23. Lower TC/RCs are reflective of a tight copper concentrate market as concentrate demand from smelters has been high and expected mine supply growth in <sup>’</sup>24 has been slashed. This matters because mine supply is the major bottleneck in copper. The tightening in the copper concentrate market has come even more quickly than we were expecting and looks at a tipping point that will eventually lead to smelter production cuts. This increased supply-led risk of a deeper refined deficit over the course of this year continues to boost our conviction in a bullish trajectory for copper prices, particularly when ex-Chinese demand begins to recover, further stressing an increasingly fragile looking copper supply outlook (Metals Weekly, Jan 19<sup>th</sup>).

## Shipping disruptions reignite supply chain woes

Disruptions to use of Suez and Panama Canals have led to a surge in global shipping costs

• Several carmakers in Europe have also announced temporary stoppages citing supply chain issues

• Rise in transport costs likely to put upward pressure on consumer goods prices

But a return to COVID-era pressures remains unlikely due to excess capacity and weaker goods demand

We estimate a 0.7%-pt add to 1H24 global core goods CPI (0.3%-pt on core) if recent cost increases persist

The conflict in the Middle East, combined with drought in Central America, have led to material disruptions in global maritime trade, adding significant delays to shipping routes along the Suez and Panama Canals. Re-routing from the Suez is increasing transport times by around a week (30% longer), which is raising costs for fuel, staffing, and insurance. As a result, global shipping costs, which had seen a near-complete reversal from pandemic-driven surges in 2021-22, have started to rise sharply again (Figure 1).

Figure 1: Container freight rate and global supply chain pressures
![](images/5015da12362b4be04ca1895d856674de2dc14a005e2acdbae0f2cf27dc71d940.jpg)

Shipping delays are also starting to disrupt supply chains. For example, several Europe-based auto plants have already announced temporary production shutdowns due to delays in obtaining car parts from Asia. The lengthening of supplier delivery times acts as an adverse supply shock that reduces excess global shipping capacity.

While such disruptions are reminiscent of what occurred earlier in the pandemic, several key differences suggest that associated pressures are likely to be more muted than experienced in 2021-22. The severe supply-demand imbalance that characterized the COVID period is no longer in place. China<sup>’</sup>s outsized excess capacity is still helping to sustain broader downward pressures on global manufactured goods prices. The profile of demand growth is at best mixed and its composition has shifted away from goods towards services. Nonetheless, as global goods disinflation has been the primary driver of lower inflation around the world, the recent reductions in global shipping capacity, at the very least, risk interrupting the disinflationary trend. At worst they will push traded goods prices higher for a period of time.

Increases in shipping costs are likely to pass through into imported goods prices with a lag and will depend on both the duration and intensity of the disruptions. The impact on prices will also depend on cyclical factors that can either reinforce or mitigate the upward pressure on prices. For instance, during a period of weaker consumer demand, a greater portion of upward pressure on shipping costs could be absorbed by profit margins than reflected in higher final output prices.

We estimate a 0.7%-pt add to global goods inflation (0.3%-pts to core inflation) during 1H24 if the recent jump in shipping costs persists. Most of that inflationary impulse is not likely to be felt until late 1Q or early 2Q, given lagged pass-through. Moreover, if sustained, there could be a hit to global industry on the downside, reinforcing our concerns that global industry is starting the year off weak and could struggle to post even a modest 1-2%ar gain in 1H24.

## A tale of two canals

Recent attacks on ships in the Southern Red Sea from Houthi rebels in Yemen have led major shipping companies, as well as some oil companies, to halt journeys through the Suez Canal. Since the first shipping companies decided to re-route around Africa in mid-December showed, Suez Canal traffic has declined 45-50% in volume terms (Figure 2).

Figure 2: Transit trade volumes
![](images/0b1513def5613651d053ab8f69f1488faa5aa8e11bdb5b585af18c915c9efd5d.jpg)

Large relative declines for both container and oil tanker transits have been recorded. However the Suez Canal is far more important for goods other than the hydrocarbon trade; the transit of crude from the Gulf is instead more reliant on the Strait of Hormuz with the use of pipelines, such as the large East-West Saudi one, as ways to bypass the chokepoint.

Adding insult to injury, the Panama Canal is facing one of the region<sup>’</sup>s worst droughts since the 1950s. The water level in Gatun Lake, which supplies water to the Canal, has dropped to its lowest level on record (Figure 3). Despite remediation measures, authorities have been forced to reduce the number of vessels crossing the Canal daily by about a third: from a typical average of about 36 to only around 24 currently. Panama shippers are paying hefty fees to move up the queue and average tolls have risen.

Figure 3: Panama Gatun lake level
![](images/5a78228e742e3b9555cd46418ad6c0e678d15b481fde52af374ae296032abf42.jpg)

These two events are adding upward pressure on transportation prices. However, each plays a distinct role in global trade and supply chains. Overall, more than 80% of global goods trade relies on sea transport with functional chokepoints key to their efficient flow. The Suez Canal accounts for about 12% global maritime trade and 30% of global container shipping trade. For comparison, only 5% of global maritime trade passes through the Panama Canal and 60% of this is dry-bulk goods (i.e., grains, minerals) as well as petroleum and petroleum products exported to Asia.

Figure 4: Suez Canal container ship transits by route
![](images/8df60acba64408893e16be8ebef91a801c48ff22c2787a33ed9c6fb4e57d3c83.jpg)

Trade between Asia and Europe comprises about 70% of Suez Canal traffic (Figure 4), whereas 73% of Panama Canal transit flows are either bound to or coming from the US and over 50% of total traffic is from the US East Coast to Asia. The re-routing of ships around Africa<sup>’</sup>s Cape of Good Hope adds 7-10 days to the journey, impacting delivery times and fuel costs. This equates to roughly a 30% increase in transit times and implies an approximately 9% reduction in effective global container shipping capacity.

Ships going from the Atlantic to the Pacific (i.e., southbound) through the Panama Canal have fared worst, with a 6.1%y/y decline in FY 2023 (Oct-Sep) tonnage transit—even before the most stringent restrictions set in place in 4Q23. Crude and liquefied natural gas tankers have taken a big hit, partly offset by liquefied petroleum gas. Still, southbound oil-related trade volumes dropped roughly 6%y/y in FY23. Dry bulk and container cargo trade have also taken a sizable hit. By contrast, northbound container cargo (to the US) rose on the margin.

Figure 5: FY 2023 trade volume by market in the Panama Canal %oya (share of total traded volume in parenthesis)
![](images/97cede7fc5a6f027b777b4085f59d016cbc0102a343e7ca4e5fc179e9a46f1c2.jpg)

On top of previously planned increases in minimum tariffs last fiscal year, firms have reportedly paid as much as a few million dollars in the Panama Canal<sup>’</sup>s limited auctions to speed up their transit. While all ships can bid for extraordinary passage through the Neopanamax lock, LPG and LNG tankers usually place the highest bids. The average revenue per ship transiting the Canal was increasing at a roughly 14%oya pace early in last year; October data (the latest available) showed it soaring 25%.

## Shipping costs and delivery times on the rise again...

The disruptions to shipping through the Suez Canal, exacerbated by traffic redirected from the Panama Canal, have recently started up push up global containerized shipping costs—although the increases are only a fraction of the surge during 2021 (Figure 6). By contrast, the Baltic Dry index—a shipping cost measure for bulk goods, including foodstuff, metals, ores, coal, etc.—has eased recently after an spike in early December. Nonetheless, the Baltic Dry Index remains 30% above its level six months ago.

Movement in the BDI likely has more of an impact on raw material and intermediate goods prices, meaning it should have a slower and potentially smaller pass-through into the prices of manufactured consumer goods than movements in containerized shipping costs. In addition, fewer of the bulk transport ships appear to have been redirected away from the

Suez Canal transit relative to container ships. As noted above, bulk goods are a significant share of Panama Canal traffic, however.

Figure 6: Global shipping costs
![](images/6dcb29209c25db0bbb01204ad40d7f6e80fa6bb83d068ed906b43414a9c2b78f.jpg)

While several aggregate measures of container shipping costs are now two-and-a-half to nearly three times their early-December levels, prices along routes that would typically go through the Suez Canal—particularly from Asia to Europe— have surged nearly four-fold (Figure 7). Costs from Europe to Asia also have more than doubled. The latest data on containerized shipping costs from Asia to the US are approaching double the levels at the start of December, with data from Frieghtos showing a slightly larger gain for routes to the East Coast (which would have to travel through the Panama Canal) than the West Coast, in contrast with the WCI data in Figure 7.

Figure 7: Recent increase in shipping costs by route
![](images/6593f7811f52834b7da17f67a7702dd65a1c32dea9326bed9bff589ae496c326.jpg)

Interestingly, prices for the reverse route—from the US East Coast to China—are up almost as much, based on Freightos data. Prior to the rise of geopolitical tensions, some of the traffic between the US East Coast and Asia that would have gone through the Panama Canal was diverted to the Suez. Other Asian exports likely have been diverted to US West Coast ports, then transported by truck or rail to the East Coast.

One way to monitor how these shipping disruptions are impacting the economy is through the PMIs. Through the

December readings (the PMI surveys cover a period from mid-month to mid-month), there is limited evidence of lengthening suppliers<sup>’</sup> delivery times (that is, a lower reading on this PMI component) as a result of these shipping dislocations. By early 2023 the indexes for both the US and Euro area had moved back into expansionary territory, suggesting a significant improvement in delivery times as recoveries gained hold. But from mid-2023 these indexes slid back toward 50, consistent with a longer-turn normalization following the pandemic (Figure 8). Should these start to deteriorate further again in coming months, that would be an indication of a more meaningful fallout to global supply chains.

Figure 8: Manufacturing PMI suppliers' delivery times DI, sa (down means longer delivery times)
![](images/c36b00f7cc93d0896c15316851e6ba079d06dd4506c91ba50934f5c6af72adbd.jpg)

## ...and could remain elevated for a while

These surges in part reflect spillovers across shipping routes as traffic gets re-routed and delayed—another sign of an effective reduction in global shipping capacity. While the largest and most direct impact so far has been along the Asiato-Europe routes, costs along other routes could also increase further as capacity moves to impacted lanes and reduces excess capacity on other lanes. These issues could be exacerbated if some orders are rolled forward in anticipation of lengthening delays, leading to increased demand from shippers on top of reduced capacity. This dynamic was observed during the earlier pandemic-induced supply-chain disruptions.

The longer the duration of these disruptions, the more likely that shipping rates stay elevated—if not increase further. Ongoing security concerns in the Red Sea mean re-routing is likely to continue until safe passage is re-established. In the Panama Canal, improving weather conditions have led authorities recently to increase the number of ships that transit the Canal each day from 22 to 24, starting in mid-January. However, this number is still well below its full capacity, and incoming data suggest significant restrictions are likely to remain in place throughout this year.

The one potential silver lining is that there remains an excess supply of container ships globally—and many that were ordered during the pandemic period (which take 2-3 years to build) continue to enter service. Thus, it appears likely that once the disruptions are over, shipping rates could revert low-

er fairly quickly.

## Pressure on goods prices: not COVID-sized

After running above our expectations for much of 1H23, goods inflation stepped down materially in 2H23—accounting for much of the slide in global core inflation (Figure 9). While the normalization in supply chains had largely run its course by late 2023, goods price pressures were expected to remain contained well into 2024, reflecting excess capacity in China (and Europe) and generally sluggish goods demand.

Against this baseline, the recent surge in global shipping costs looks set to pressure consumer price inflation higher in coming months. While we do not expect a rise anywhere near as large as the COVID-era shock, even a modest rebound in goods inflation could render global core CPI inflation sticky around the 3% mark, especially if services inflation stays stuck above central bank targets as we project.

Figure 9: Global core CPI - ex Chn and Tur %3m saar
![](images/a363ddfdf0f8214fb717ae6fa07992d63812defc11d8a1383cb9a8803d7247e4.jpg)

Unlike during the COVID and Russia invasion shocks, global manufacturing output is not being curtailed by governmentimposed lockdowns. Rather, the main impact is through a reduction in container shipping capacity that is delaying delivery of some inputs to manufacturing production, primarily from Asian suppliers to Europe. Moreover, global goods demand is not currently surging and the composition of demand has shifted away from goods and back to services (Figure 10). And in contrast to 2021-22, China<sup>’</sup>s outsized excess capacity is currently helping to sustain downward pressure on global manufactured goods prices.

Figure 10: Global real GDP, decomposed relative to potential Index, 4Q19=100
![](images/bdb609ebde08d9d82456f0d082560d02fb81ae4053a772014a4242742239e823.jpg)

## Models confirm goods CPI lift

Over the past few years we have looked at the New York Fed<sup>’</sup>s global supply chain pressure index (GSCPI) as a simple tool to measure the inflationary impact of supply chain dislocations. The GSCPI conditions change in a range of shipping prices (BDI, Harpex index, and US airfreight cost indices) as well as supply chain-related components from selected economies<sup>’</sup> PMI surveys (delivery times, backlogs, and purchased stocks) on PMI new orders and quantities purchased (to control for customer demand and intermediate inputs) in an attempt to isolate supply disturbances from demand effects.

After falling into negative territory in the first three quarters of 2023, the GSCPI moved back up to zero as of November-December to suggest that supply chain pressures have reverted to their historical norms. (Figure 11; the index is measured as a standard deviation from its 1997-2021 average.) It does not incorporate the spot container rates that have moved up sharply since December, and we assume the GSCPI will rise further over 1H24, reflecting a lagged impact of those increases on the other variables in the index.

Figure 11: Supply chain pressures and core goods inflation
![](images/e23aac2cf1094f32c796646159841b693dafeb07af57789021a0ee64a33fee43.jpg)

Our model that forecasts global core goods price inflation based on the GSCPI suggests last quarter will mark a bottom for core goods price inflation with a return to modest positive gains during in 1H24 (Figure 12). This assumes the GSCPI will rise to around 0.5 in 1H24, in line with 2017-19 average, but well below its 2021-22 levels.

Figure 12: Global core goods CPI %q/q, saar, shaded area is J.P. Morgan forecast
![](images/5233062a3986a781006d3b42edc56251539b114af3efcef846c99bbe1b1d764e.jpg)

Although the GSCPI is designed to identify supply disturbances, it does not incorporate information from the previously-discussed shipping cost measures that have been most sensitive to the recent disruptions facing the Canals. We thus compliment our analysis by examining the relationship between shipping costs and CPI goods price inflation.

We regress monthly changes in global core goods CPI on its own lag and lags of various shipping cost indicators (individually). We also control for demand by including the PMI purchases index. We find an a 0.7%-pt increase in core goods CPI inflation in response to a roughly 150% sustained increase in container shipping costs (average of first three rows in Table 1). Given an approx. 40% weigh on goods in global core CPI, this implies a 0.27%-pt add to global core CPI.

Table 1: Impact of shipping cost increase on global CPI
<table><tr><td>Indicator</td><td>3m* chg (%)</td><td>Lag to CPI</td><td>%-pt add to core goods CPI</td></tr><tr><td>Freightos Global</td><td>144</td><td>4m</td><td>0.64</td></tr><tr><td>Shanghai Container</td><td>147</td><td>4m</td><td>0.86</td></tr><tr><td>Drewry WCI</td><td>176</td><td>3m</td><td>0.64</td></tr><tr><td>Baltic Dry index</td><td>30</td><td>3m</td><td>0.05</td></tr><tr><td>Harpex Index</td><td>0</td><td>8m</td><td>0.00</td></tr></table>

Source: J.P. Morgan estimates, Bloomberg. Regresses %m/m core goods CPI on lagged CPI, shipping cost indicator and manufacturing PMI purchases; Jan 2017-Sep 2023. \*6m for Baltic Dry

These simple regressions suggest a lag of around four months from the increase in the shipping cost measure to the most significant impact on global core goods inflation, suggesting a peak impulse in late 1Q24. Across the most widely used shipping cost indicators, the Harpex index passes through to prices more slowly (perhaps reflecting the slower diffusion of shipping dislocations through charter prices), with the most significant impact after 8 months. Our analysis also points to a much smaller impact from the Baltic Dry index on global goods inflation, which is intuitive given the impact of changes in the shipping costs of bulk commodities upon consumer goods prices should be smaller than for intermediate goods prices.

A preliminary look at some of the country-level relationships points to a broadly similar core goods CPI impact for the US and the Euro area, albeit with a much longer pass-through to consumer prices in the Euro area (four quarters) than in the US (1-2 quarters). That said, for the US, the overall core CPI impact may be tempered somewhat by a lower share of core goods in the US core CPI basket (27%) relative to the Euro area (37%) and EM (45%).

South East Asian economies could also see spillovers to prices from the knock-on effects to intra-regional shipping costs. However, the greatest exposure for EM Asian consumers would be through higher costs of importing food and energy goods from the US (via the Panama Canal). While this scenario also poses some upside risks to headline inflation, we note that global commodity price indices, including for agricultural goods, continued to decline through late last year, thanks in part to ample inventories. Moreover, EM policymakers would likely put in place measures to limit domestic price pressures, as they did in response to last year<sup>’</sup>s adverse weather shocks.

## Germany: Assessing fiscal policy in 2024

• German fiscal policy was thrown into disarray by last year's Constitutional Court ruling

• The government had to pass a supplementary budget for 2023 and rework the 2024 budget

• A number of measures will lift inflation directly: they add up to around 1%-pt, but not all are new

• The drag on growth may still only be similar to before: at most 0.5% of GDP

Last year<sup>’</sup>s ruling of the German Federal Constitutional Court (GFCC) has forced the government to pass a supplementary budget for 2023 and to rework its draft 2024 budget. The implications are large for the conduct of German fiscal policy, which has been working around the very tight constraint of the debt brake with the use of special funds and reserves. The GFCC ruling put a halt on this practice, which may improve the transparency of German fiscal policy going forward.

The new budgets include some measures that directly impact inflation in 2024. They may add up to 1%-pt on German HICP inflation. But, some of these were already known and have only shifted in timing, while other factors will also provide some offset (especially the past reductions in wholesale gas and electricity prices that get passed through with a lag). As a result, the net effect in the January print will be important to see. In terms of the growth impact, assessments are also still fluid, but we suspect the drag on growth was at most 0.5% of GDP last year and that it will be similar again this year. This implies that the GFCC ruling may not have materially amplified the growth impact.

## A long list of inflationary measures

The GFCC ruling has added some measures with a direct inflationary impact and adds to others there already due to come into effect early this year.

VAT on restaurant meals returns to 19%. In October 2022, the VAT rate on restaurant meals was reduced from 19% to 7% as one of the COVID-support measures. With weights of 3.6% and 5.5% in the CPI and HICP, respectively, the full price increase would boost headline inflation by 0.4%-pt and 0.6%-pt, respectively. But this overstates the effect as not all meals and drinks are covered (e.g. takeaways) and as the pass-through tends to be incomplete with profit margins likely to absorb some of the higher VAT rate. We assume half of the full effects, 0.2%-pt and 0.3%-pt, respectively.

• VAT on gas and district heat returns to 19%. As one of the energy-support measures, the VAT rate on gas and district heat was reduced from 19% to 7%. This measure reversed this month, three months earlier than initially planned. With weights of around 1.5%, the effect on headline inflation would be almost 0.2%-pt.

Gas and electricity price brakes end. Since the start of 2023, 80% of households<sup>’</sup> normal gas and electricity usage was capped at a price of €12/MWh and €40/MWh, respectively. These price brakes were ended this month, rather than in April, so that customers on expensive deals may now pay their full contractual price already in January. Unfortunately, it is hard to know how existing contracts are priced and when they come up for renewal. But, some upward pressure on prices paid is likely as some customers will still be on older contracts at higher prices. Over time, those contracts will reset lower again as suggested by the Verivox new contract prices that are already at or below the price brake levels (Figure 1).

Figure 1: German gas and electricity bills Cent/kWh
![](images/215a30cb71279fca7d9ac3f86384b1fefb12ec6a34e746c66dc7711801f144e0.jpg)

A 50% increase in the price of $\mathbf { C O } _ { 2 } .$ Germany puts a price per ton on $\mathrm { C O } _ { 2 }$ on petrol, diesel and heating oil, and is now expanding this to coal in electricity generation. The prices are also being raised from €30/ton to €45/ton. These increases are larger than initially planned due to the GFCC ruling. The impact of the CO is not straightforward to estimate as it cuts across a wide range of goods. But, the Bundesbank<sup>’</sup>s estimate appears reasonable. This implies a positive impact on headline HICP inflation of 0.25%-pt in 2024, 0.15%-pt in 2025 and 0.1%-pt in 2026. The increases in 2025 and 2026 are due to further increases in the CO price to €55/ton in 2025 and possibly to €65/ton in 2026. In terms of the most direct effect, petrol/ diesel prices per litre may rise 2.5% in January, giving almost a 0.1%-pt contribution to headline HICP.

Higher network charges for electricity. As a direct consequence of the GFCC ruling, the government is no longer subsidizing network charges, which have doubled this month to €0.06/MWh. This would raise electricity bills by 7.5%, contributing 0.2%-pt to headline inflation.

A number of other tax changes. The lorry toll on motorways is being increased and expanded, leading to extra revenues of perhaps €8bn in 2024 (0.2% of GDP). This will also raise consumer inflation, although the magnitude is not easy to pin down. A reasonable assumption is an effect on headline inflation of almost 0.1%-pt. A tax on domestic flights is also being raised, although their share in the CPI and HICP is well under 1%. A tax on plastics is due in 2025.

Minimum wage rose 3.4% this month. After the large increases in 2022, the minimum wage was held steady last year, but rose by 3.4% at the start of this year. It is scheduled to rise by another 3.3% at the start of 2025. Other wages in the economy are still rising faster than this and that will also reinforce some further momentum, especially in some services categories.

The list of upward price impacts is long and the 2024 impact from the measures listed above is large at around 1%-pt on headline inflation. Some of this will impact via core inflation: the VAT on restaurants, the CO price and the higher lorry toll. The effects on core could be 0.6%pts, but not immediately in January.

But, what makes the overall effects harder to gauge is that other factors are also in motion. For example, the effect on petrol/diesel prices has materialized in the weekly fuel pump data in early January, but more than half of the increase in the tax wedge was offset by a reduction in the price of oil itself. Similarly, the Verivox data for early January show outright declines in new gas and electricity tariffs despite the large increases in the gas VAT and electricity network charge. This is likely because energy suppliers are still passing on past declines in wholesale gas and electricity prices. What the Verivox data do not capture is the average bill paid by existing customers, which are likely to rise due to the increases in VAT, surcharge and the expiry of the price brakes. Other effects will take time to pass through such as the higher lorry toll rates. And, finally, the shipping disruptions in the Red Sea may create additional pressures.

Overall, we currently see German HICP inflation averaging 2.8%oya in 2024, with January printing at 3.7%oya. But, given the large number of policy impacts at the start of the year, it will be important to see the actual print in January. The same applies to core, which we see averaging 2.7%oya in 2024 and printing at 3.4%oya in January. Risks on core may be on the upside, but this will be easier to assess after the January data.

## The impact on growth

A key problem in Germany<sup>’</sup>s fiscal policy-making is that most of the discussion is around the federal budget in the

Finance Statistics, which the debt brake refers to, while the more meaningful assessments are based on the General Government balances in the National Accounts definitions.

In terms of the National Accounts, the statistics office estimated this week that the general government deficit improved by 0.5%-pt in 2023 to 2.0%. This is a much smaller deficit than the government was projecting in early 2023. There is no official estimate yet for 2024, but Finance Minister Lindner has suggested a likely deficit of around 1.5% of GDP, while the Bundesbank assumes 1.3% (Figure 2). These estimates include the measures already announced in the supplementary budget for 2023 and the new draft budget for 2024.

Figure 2: Germany government deficit % of GDP, box is Bundesbank estimate for 2024
![](images/da3bbb8b5a9988f519909a81147213178ceebfd4e04af6816822312f29614b26.jpg)

If we take the standard EU fiscal framework, a fiscal deficit of 1.3% this year would require an improvement of the structural primary balance of around 0.7%-pt of GDP. This would be a smaller tightening than the 1.1%-pt of GDP in 2023. But, as we have noted before, this standard methodology for assessing the stance of fiscal policy has not sufficiently differentiated during the pandemic and energy-crisis between cyclical and structural effects. Based on Bundesbank estimates, a large part of the improvement in the structural primary balance in 2023 was due to fading crisis-related measures and the same will occur this year (Figure 4). Hence, the effective impact on growth is likely significantly smaller than these estimates suggest as much of the tightening has come from the phasing out of crisis-related measures. Taking the 1.1%-pt improvement in the structural primary balance in 2023 as an upper limit, we suspect the growth impact was likely at most 0.5% of GDP. This implies a fiscal multiplier of below 0.5. Hence, the German economy<sup>’</sup>s underperformance last year did not primarily owe to fiscal drags.

Figure 3: German structural primary budget balance %-pt change from 2019
![](images/571f29ade2e2f4ac139563f83209ce08b2079c25fd112178e6e5f3521e418e05.jpg)

For this year, the situation is even harder to assess. Lindner<sup>’</sup>s statement about a 1.5% general government deficit implies a structural tightening of only 0.7% of GDP and all of that would come from an expiry of energy-related measures. The latter points to a limited growth effect. For example, the ending of the gas and electricity price brakes may save the government 1% of GDP compared to 2023, although the fiscal cost was already reducing in the course of 2023 as more and more customers refinanced their deals at lower prices.

But, how can this be reconciled with the debt brake requirement? The debt brake limits the federal structural deficit to 0.35% of GDP and the Bundesbank<sup>’</sup>s assumption of an output gap of over 1% of GDP may increase this for the general government deficit toward 1.0% of GDP, if an estimate close to this is used for the debt brake calculation. That still leaves a gap to the general government deficit of 1.5% suggested by Lindner.

Nevertheless, assessments of the size of the budget hole from the perspective of the debt brake are fluid. For exmaple, the Kiel Institute has already revised down its assessment of the additional gap caused by the GFCC ruling from €28bn to €21bn, i.e. to just 0.5% of GDP. And it notes that a large part will be filled with reduced transfers that may have a low impact on growth. In brief, as more information becomes available, it appears that the government is finding ways to reduce the gap and plug what remains in ways that weigh less on the economy. Last year<sup>’</sup>s fiscal outperformance may also help via an unused reserve. Overall, we suspect that German fiscal policy will still be a drag on growth of at most 0.5% of GDP, despite the GFCC ruling, but still with significant uncertainty. But, even this may require some further measures to be added at some point.

In terms of debt, this looks set to continue its downward path. Having increased to 69.0% of GDP in 2021, it has already fallen to below 65% by mid-2023 and could reach 62% in 2024. This highlights that the German fiscal “crisis<sup>”</sup> has largely been self-inflicted, driven by political pressure to meet a self-imposed and arguably overly-strict domestic debt

brake. The EU-level rules would require less of an adjustment.

## Brazil is still positioned to grow

• We see GDP growth accelerating in early 2024 and then sustaining a near 2%ar pace

• Private sector balances suggest growth should remain solid

Private savings, profit rates and wealth remain above the pre-pandemic levels

• Strained fiscal accounts and external risks could lead to weakness ahead

We forecast GDP growth to move from around 3%y/y last year to 1.6% this year. This masks an important sequential reacceleration of growth in early 2024, which we expect to be sustained thereafter. After almost no expansion in the second half of last year – that generates a statistical carry-over close to stability to next year<sup>’</sup>s GDP – we expect GDP growth to reaccelerate to above 2%q/q saar during the next four quarters (Figure 1).

Figure 1: Quarterly GDP growth
![](images/0dd7ddb1d43f49b9e763e12444dac5e1b54418e60c32a5a42a7a06332d893dc4.jpg)

Figure 2: Oil production Mbpd
![](images/88ae4eb2774ef95354b7d544855e297dce0a79778f1016ed7bd2ac3e2dcf65df.jpg)

In our view, Brazil is cyclically well positioned to grow. Strength of aggregate balance sheet of households, companies and banks – partly at the expense of a worse government balance sheet – explains some of the solid growth in non-cyclical sectors of the economy recently. With private sector balance sheets remaining solid, improvements in supply-side condi-

tions (e.g. oil sector, Figure 2) and the (albeit limited) impulse from the precatórios payment, growth should remain resilient.

## Boom, payback and normalization

In this first section of this note, we discuss why last year<sup>’</sup>s growth performance was strong and concentrated in the first half of the year. 2023 was marked by strong and higher-thanexpected growth despite restrictive monetary policy stance. However, the aggregate performance masks sectoral divergence.

The more cyclical sectors of the economy – such as manufacturing, construction and retail sales – decelerated substantially and may end up growing less than 1% last year. This shows that tight monetary policy is working through the economy, at least to some extent. Meanwhile, the non-cyclical sectors of the economy have accelerated, led by double-digit agricultural output gains. But even excluding agricultural output, less cyclical sectors (e.g. mining, financial intermediation) have expanded between 3%-4% (Figure 3).

Figure 3: Value added by component Index, 4Q19=100. Share of 2022 GDP in parenthesis
![](images/3ebc73ab63b0fe0fc899e1d7929ab5848022a8e5786f57cb88d65da8b9748bea.jpg)

This divergence also affected the performance throughout the quarters of last year. The agricultural boom, concentrated at the beginning of the year by rising 60%q/q saar in 1Q, boosted GDP to grow on average 5%q/q saar in the first half of 2023. In contrast, this crop concentration led agriculture to contribute negatively to GDP growth in the second part of last year. Outside of agriculture, value-added output accelerated from around 1% in 1Q to close to 4% in 2Q and then 2.4% in 3Q – with private consumption growing around 4%q/q saar in the year through 3Q23. The end of this larger-than-usual influence of agriculture to GDP<sup>’</sup>s quarterly performance in 2024 should give more clarity of how solid and sustained this economic growth cycle is.

## Saving, profiting, lending

With that in mind, the main reason we see economic growth resilience this year is the health of private sector balance sheets, which helped GDP remain strong last year, even outside the agricultural boom. Our colleagues have discussed how EM economies avoided growth breaking down during this economic cycle, despite the marked tightening of financial conditions. That account places significant weight on the precondition of solid private sector balance sheets and the absence of extended spending relative to private sector incomes. We believe these factors remain encouraging for Brazil<sup>’</sup>s 2024 growth.

The leverage of the private sector does not look elevated by historical standards. After a decline over 2021 into 2022, the private sector savings rate, proxied by the difference between the economy total savings minus the public sector result, has increased significantly through 2023 (Figure 4). The private sector seems to have used the opportunity of larger fiscal deficits with high real interest rates to save more than usual, funds that in turn can be used in the future if labor market conditions start to deteriorate.

Figure 4: Private savings rate
![](images/65c7f564fd70feba1fc44109861d67edde22401bd88db76dad63b7174d5b3635.jpg)

Also, profit margins of listed companies continue to run above the pre-pandemic level and the historical average, levels that are historically consistent with solid job creation (Figure 5). This data also suggests a slowdown in employment growth, as does the historically low level of the unemployment rate. However, we expect the unemployment rate to move modestly higher during 2024, as we see labor participation partially normalizing, helping easing inflationary pressure too.

%, from MSCI Brazil, dotted lines are net formal jobs, 12-month sum, millions historical averages

![](images/e608bc1e3a9ebefa15f4cb28ed398f112f6b6d3991bc9c937b843badee7b58be.jpg)

This profit margin data only reflects the state of listed companies, which are not representative of the overall economy. While it shows the situation for larger companies, we have little clarity about the situation of smaller enterprises, a concern given that the pandemic had asymmetric impact in different sectors and companies.

Smaller firms are often dependent on banks for funding, and it does not seem to be the case that banks are under pressure to delever. Our proxy for loans-to-deposit ratio is around the historical average and significantly below the pre-pandemic level (Figure 6). This helps to explain why household loan growth has remained relatively high, despite the rapid and extensive tightening of interest rates. Lending to companies has fallen marginally in real terms, but the use of financial markets as a source of financing is again making up for part of this slowdown (Figure 7). Now that interest rates and nonperforming loans from households are starting to come down, this source of drag to the economy should start to ease going forward.

Figure 5: Profit margin and job creation
Figure 6: Loans-to-deposit ratio
%, credit operations outstanding was used as a proxy for total loans before 2013
![](images/44187f7332b40c07bc16bd8c4c1de80817254a7d038aea3d24d508667dbf8a4e.jpg)

Figure 7: Outstanding credit and private bonds
![](images/b130a3c75030db1f169f26f1d213886abeb05c64f2f503424208c6f4d9177f30.jpg)

## Pressure points

We see private balance sheets as solid,helping the Brazilian economy to keep growing this year, but we recognize that there are some pressure points that could impact economic growth. While it seems that relative leverage of the private sector is low, the flow is worrisome, particularly for households: income commitment with debt payments has been around the historical highs in the last couple of years. A hiccup in the labor market should lead consumers to cut spending to preserve the capacity to repay these loans.

This income commitment is a factor that has been worrying us for some time and one of the factors behind our less upbeat views in the past. However, even with policy rates peaking close to 14%, there was no significant disruption coming out of this risk. With policy rates 200bp lower and set to decrease further, lending rates are falling, and so is income commitment with debt payments (Figure 8).

Household balances remained preserved for a variety of reasons, but chiefly at the expense of a worsening in the public sector balance, especially last year. We estimate that the fiscal transfers, which were historically around 10% of the overall disposable income, have recently been running at 15%. If the public sector, with one of the highest debt-to-GDP across EM economies, is required to adjust, this would have an impact on incomes that can also drag growth.

Finally, if the global economy performs significantly worse than expected, this will impact Brazil. But with global financial conditions improving and an increasing likelihood of a global soft landing, and the solid private balance sheets in Brazil, we see the economy poised to show another year of solid growth.

Figure 8: Indebtedness and income commitment with debt payments % of last 12-month disposable income 3-month as % of disposable income, sa
![](images/075412306d67d2f998a8deaa09e6bb751fa25e43ccb4abb6daf6ca731f4b5f38.jpg)
Source: BCB

## BoE will need more persuasion to cut in 1H24

Inflation is undershooting BoE forecasts, putting pressure on the Bank to ease earlier than planned

We now look for the first cut to come in August, but the MPC will need more persuasion to consider 1H24

BoE will remain concerned about underlying inflation, and needs time to rebuild confidence

Inflation surprises have swung mainly been to the downside of late, raising optimism about a soft landing and the prospect of early BoE rate cuts. In response to this we now see the Bank cutting a little earlier (August instead of November) this year, taking rates down 75bps to 4.5% by year end. But we have not changed our end-2025 forecast of 3%, and remain skeptical about the likelihood of a 1H24 start to easing.

Lower inflation might support early rate cuts when viewed through the lens of a particular objective for real rates, or within a standard Taylor Rule framework. But there are several reasons why the BoE is likely to be resistant to this idea. Chief among them is the need for the BoE to rebuild its confidence that inflation really is on a path that takes inflation fully back to target.

Given the bruising experience of 1H23, and the ongoing stickiness in domestic measures of inflation, this is likely to take time. Even with headline inflation set to briefly drop below target this year, the BoE is likely to continue erring on the side of caution with rates. Early pay indications for 2024 will be key in influencing its upcoming policy decisions, and the Bank will be more hesitant to cut if pay awards come in close to 5% or higher.

While the odds of a soft landing appear to have increased, there is still no guarantee that this will be the end game for inflation. So while it is reasonable to expect an earlier start to easing than the BoE previously envisaged, the Bank is likely to remain cautious. On the argument for cutting early to offset the rise in real rates of late, expectations of nominal rates have already declined alongside the drop in inflation and this is buying the BoE some time. Cutting early could prompt markets to price in a more aggressive easing cycle which the BoE may find counterproductive.

In this note we also make a 0.4%-pt upgrade to our growth forecast for this year, reflecting stronger real incomes and easier financial conditions. Our baseline forecast still assumes that the inflation fight will require some additional help from a couple of quarters of negative growth along the way, requiring monetary policy to remain restrictive. But a somewhat

better near term growth outlook would present the MPC with another reason for not jumping the gun: even a modest recovery in activity could halt the process of labour market loosening and further complicate a sustained return of inflation back to 2%.

## Inflation on track to drop below target in 2Q

Core inflation on our forecast remains above target and this should keep the BoE mindful of the difficulties in achieving the <sup>‘</sup>last mile<sup>’</sup> of its inflation journey. Nevertheless, a 15% drop in regulated domestic energy bills in April looks set to shave 0.6%-pts off the CPI and pull headline inflation at least briefly below target. This undershoot only lasts for a period of six months in our forecast, and shouldn<sup>’</sup>t necessarily make a big difference for a central bank more focussed on domestic pressures. But the BoE had assumed a large degree of persistence in inflation, forecasting that the headline CPI would still be running at 3.6%oya in 2Q24. Not only is this unlikely to play out, but the optics of even a brief episode of sub-target inflation is likely to place additional pressure for it to start cutting this year. Note, however, that inflation is not likely to fall below target until April. And as the data for that month will not be released until the 22nd May, this will come after the BoE meeting on the 9th of that month.

## Reasons for MPC caution

Notwithstanding the undershoot in headline, there are both tactical and data considerations that we think will make the BoE reluctant to begin easing as early as 1H24.

MPC confidence building. The MPC<sup>’</sup>s current rhetoric has been more hawkish than the Fed, with three dissents for higher rates at the December meeting and no dovish change to its language. This may owe in part to UK inflation decoupling from the global pack during 1H23, creating concern at the BoE. Those upside risks are fading now, but it will probably take the MPC some time to regain confidence that this is a sustained development. Taylor rules have been less useful since inflation surged, as they are usually based on spot rates which nobody real ly expects to persist. Moreover, Taylor Rule estimates can divergence significantly from the official policy rate. But if the BoE did cut in 3Q, this would only be a quarter later than when our own Taylor Rule estimate falls below the current 5.25% policy rate. This difference is small given the series history, and understandable given a cautious central bank. But in any case an alternative version based on core inflation is much more closely aligned with our latest policy rate forecast (Figure 1).

![](images/9e07b00246b6d430cb753b63db031dee1a97b26172cade162279199574e85fd5.jpg)
Source: J.P. Morgan calculations

Services inflation remains sticky. Goods inflation has accounted for the bulk of the slowing in core in recent months. Service inflation, which more closely mirrors domestic inflation pressures, has slowed from its extreme highs but nevertheless remains firm at 6.4%oya. While below the BoE<sup>’</sup>s November forecast, progress remains slow as the higher frequency 3m run rate remains too high for the BoE at 5.2% annualized. A sustainable return of inflation to the Bank<sup>’</sup>s target would likely require this run rate to move back closer to 2.5% or so. For the reasons discussed below around the labour market, we do not expect services inflation to approach a run rate similar to that this year. We assume a 4.0-4.5% average, and the recent rise in the price balance of the PMI appears to hint that the disinflation process is slowing down (Figure 2). If core goods price gains run close to 1%, this would leave core inflation overall running at close to 3% on a sequential basis. It is hence too early to declare victory and for the BoE to ease very early or quickly.

![](images/ffa6bdd69af7f19742de1a3933e7b11c0d9007db9dd10199809736d75bef63f4.jpg)
Source: ONS

Wage growth remains too high. There is a strong correlation between wage growth and services inflation. Even with services inflation having slowed below expectations in recent months, the BoE is likely to remain uneasy in the absence of firmer evidence of slower pay growth. Average weekly earnings growth is down from its extreme high of 8% (orange line, Figure 3) and the three month annualized change is running at just 2.5% in the private sector. But this series has been volatile, and the very latest November data show a strong 0.6% rebound over the month. Our sense is that underlying wage growth will settle close to 5%oya by the middle of 2024, which would be too high for the MPC. Some of the less volatile measures of the underlying wage process appear to support this expected stickiness. Pay settlements, for example, were still running close to 6%oya late last year and have shown no sign of a sustained slowing (Figure 3, blue line).

Figure 3: Measures of wage growth and CPI services inflation
% oya, 3mma average earnings excludes bonus payments
![](images/bd26371a27d0e0f729bdccedc860cfb601c59526ef658db26a201599dca92e19.jpg)

Forward looking pay measures provide limited reassurance. The BoE has for a while focussed on the weak forward looking signal on pay being sent by the REC report (Figure 4, blue line). But that survey has not tracked pay outturns as well since the pandemic, and the message about year ahead wage expectations being sent by the BoE<sup>’</sup>s own DMP survey of businesses is very different (Figure 4, green line). Having been more closely aligned with recent outturns over its short history, the DMP is signalling that annual pay growth will come in at just over 5% in 2024. The BoE has tended to pay attention to expected pay awards from its Agents survey, which is due to be announced alongside the February policy report. In December, the MPC<sup>’</sup>s minutes noted that early indications show a drop below 6%. But unless that indicator comes in close to or below the BoE<sup>’</sup>s 4.25% forecast for average earnings in 2024, and hence weaker than indicated by the DMP, we would expect the Bank will remain cautious about an early 1H24 easing.

% change over previous period, annualized

Figure 4: Forward looking measures of wage growth
% bal., sa, permanent salaries %oya, 3mma, ex. bonuses, private sector, AWE
![](images/974249134ea34acd8ae3c31c99775fcc4bc02b69026112185e69a9ce91ffd617.jpg)
Source: ONS, KPMG, REC, BoE

Growth outlook looking better. Last summer we revised down our growth forecast to show falling GDP in the second half of this year. Since then, growth has been weak but the economy has avoided a recession. The forward looking picture also looks somewhat brighter than before. Wage growth remains elevated and the faster decline in inflation points to real income growth remaining close to 3% annualized in 1H24. In addition to this, financial conditions have eased significantly with terminal rates now priced 200bps lower at around 3%. This is pulling new mortgage rates lower, and is likely to support consumption. In response to this we are revising up our 2024 growth forecast by a cumulative 0.4%-pts (Table 1). The profile continues to show incremental weakness building though time, in lagged response to past hikes. But an extended period of resilience in near term growth should also reduce the urgency for the BoE to cut rates.

Source: J.P. Morgan
