## Inflation

## Japan

• BoJ to walk a fine line next week, with statement wording in focus

● Expecting eventual shift in forward guidance, confirming policy is on a gradual normalization path

• Renewed yen depreciation adding some impetus to this dynamic

• Next week: January Tokyo core CPI firms, flash PMI up modestly

We look for the BoJ to walk a fine line at next week<sup>’</sup>s policy meeting. We think the focus will be on any language changes in the accompanying statement, as well as Ueda<sup>’</sup>s tone at the press conference, with a potential adjustment to forward guidance ahead of a mid-year NIRP exit. As has been our longstanding view, we think it premature to expect any policy change next week. But given building momentum for higher wage growth ahead of the Shunto spring wage negotiations, we expect the BoJ will want to show it is still moving—albeit gradually—toward the exit door.

That the yen is now only a few percent shy of its 2023 low against the USD, and is not far off levels that prompted FX intervention, which adds some impetus to this dynamic. We have long argued that the mix of higher inflation and a slowmoving BoJ could spell trouble for the yen in the form of accelerating household capital outflows. And we remain skeptical that the BoJ<sup>’</sup>s gradual normalization path will allow Japan<sup>’</sup>s real policy rate to rise fast enough to catalyze durable yen appreciation alone. The immediate focus at next week<sup>’</sup>s policy meeting will be a likely downward revision to the FY24 official core inflation projections on the back of lower global energy prices. But to the extent that yen depreciation continues, the risk is of a second round of inflation pressure later this year.

In this context, next week<sup>’</sup>s Tokyo CPI report for January will be important in signalling whether service prices are also reaccelerating despite a backdrop of moderating domestic demand growth. We look for a brisk 0.2%m/m sa rise in the global standard measure of core prices, alongside a modest uptick in the January flash manufacturing PMI survey.

## BoJ focus to be on statement language

In the policy statement, the following three points will be the focus:

Strong easing bias. “The Bank … will not hesitate to take additional easing measures if necessary<sup>”</sup>: We think this strong easing bias in the last paragraph of the forward guidance needs to be revised well before a policy rate

hike, and expect the decision to be made at the next meeting at the earliest. The extent to which changes are accompanied by a bias toward policy tightening will be a test for measuring the distance to an eventual NIRP removal.

Policy framework of QQE with YCC. “The Bank will continue with QQE with YCC, aiming to achieve the price stability target, as long as it is necessary for maintaining that target in a stable manner<sup>”</sup>: The BoJ may not need to change this sentence until the Bank changes the current policy framework, stating its confidence regarding achievement of the target has risen sufficiently.

Expansion of monetary base. “It (the Bank) will continue expanding the monetary base until the yoy rate of increase in the observed CPI (ex. fresh food) exceeds 2% and stays above the target in a stable manner<sup>”</sup>: When the current forward guidance was introduced, the BoJ would have assumed a change in inflation environment such that the above conditions for the YCC framework would be met before inflation stayed above target for long. However, contrary to the assumption, inflation has exceeded 2% for more than 1.5 years now, removing the legitimacy of maintaining this forward guidance, but the BoJ has yet to gain confidence regarding sustainable achievement of the target. In addition, given the current market environment that has led the BoJ to reduce Rinban operations, the BoJ may prefer to introduce flexibility in its JGB purchases. We expect this sentence likely will be revised in coming meetings.

Another policy revision that the BoJ likely will make ahead of the NIRP removal is elimination of the 10Y JGB target of 0%. At the September post-meeting press conference, Gover nor Ueda stated that the YCC framework would be maintained until “the prospects for achieving the target become clear<sup>”</sup>, while short-term rates would be kept negative until “the overall outlook is achieved<sup>”</sup>. We think this differentiation suggests the BoJ<sup>’</sup>s plan is to remove the YCC “framework<sup>”</sup> ahead of the NIRP removal, although the definition of YCC framework has already become unclear. While the BoJ apparently intends to keep some controls on 10Y JGB yields for the time being, the Bank likely will claim an exit from YCC by abolishing the target of 0% once it is determined that the outlook is clear. We now believe this will be an event in spring after the wage negotiation outcome becomes clearer, as the decline in upward pressure on global yields has given the BoJ a grace period.

In the outlook report, the BoJ may revise down its CPI (ex. fresh food) outlook for FY2024, reflecting the recent decline in global oil prices, as reported by Bloomberg. This may be seen as a factor to reduce the BoJ<sup>’</sup>s confidence in achieving the target, but we believe it is actually the opposite. The BoJ<sup>’</sup>s major concern is the risk that a virtuous cycle of inflation and wages will not fully take hold due to continued weakness in domestic demand. As the downward revision is likely to lead to an upward revision of real wage prospects, we think this would instead give the BoJ some confidence in the sustainability of the recent change in inflation dynamics.

## Sentiment softens in January

Sentiment among large manufacturers fell 6 points in January, reversing December<sup>’</sup>s rise, and putting the index back at its November level of 6 (Figures 1 and 2). The decline in manufacturing sentiment looks to be a reflection of falling confidence in export-oriented auto and electronics sectors. Auto sector sentiment fell a large 31 points, leaving confidence at its lowest level in eight months (Figure 3). Some precision machinery producers cited the impact of the earthquake that struck Japan on New Year<sup>’</sup>s Day as one drag on activity. But this does not appear to have translated into a broad-base decline in sentiment. Despite the headline index decline, the share of manufacturing sectors reporting positive sentiment broadened to 82%, its highest since end-2021.

Figure 1: Japan Reuters Tankan large firms sentiment - Headline DI, level of conditions, boxes denote 3-mo outlook
![](images/b3ea5334cc935458fa39e9e043be31ce3c878f617139fc95b39eb4cb79f0ff5e.jpg)

Figure 2: Japan Reuters Tankan large firms sentiment - Headline diffusion % of sub-sectors reporting positive sentiment indices Non-mfg
![](images/e2705b4fee8756bc04c96414a996ad4cd199eecb6dd9efe63a0ae5ef04e1f010.jpg)

Non-manufacturing sentiment remains resilient. The headline index rose 3 points to 29, with all sectors reporting positive conditions. There were sustained rises in wholesale and retail sentiment indices, with the latter up 11 points to 44, its highest level since August. While the relationship between retail sentiment and spending is loose, the very high level of retail confidence relative to its long-run trend implies solid activity (Figure 4).

Figure 3: Japan Retail sentiment and consumption
![](images/8b20c39d63caf7bd3cb41f0e32d1e752055523e008bd72e90d39df9a483bb62e.jpg)

Figure 4: Japan Reuters Tankan, autos and transport equipment DI, level of conditions
![](images/5ef75435c2a87fce80bff86a80a5046cd34786a5178c1a863ff140a7d3847bc5.jpg)

Outlook indices remain close to current levels, implying a modest 2-point improvement in manufacturing confidence against a 5-point normalization in services and other nonmanufacturing sentiment.

## Services still sticky in December CPI

The December nationwide CPI report showed the second consecutive monthly moderation in headline measures, but underlying measures remained firm. The global core CPI (ex. food and energy) rose to 2.8%oya in December, with a 2.3% rise in services prices and a 3.9% rise in core goods prices. The core goods prices inflation (ex. food and energy) had been trending down since April 2023, after having hit the peak at 5.1%, due to moderation of imported cost pressures and base effects, but still showed some stickiness, rising to 3.9% in December from 3.8% in November.

Services prices continued to show solid momentum, particularly for measures excluding rent, such as accommodation, transportation, and culture and recreation. We think today<sup>’</sup>s result showed persistent price pressures in underlying inflation, reflecting the ongoing changes in firms<sup>’</sup> price setting behavior which likely will lead a virtuous cycle of inflation and wages.

Despite the recent moderation in headline measures, we continue to expect the nationwide core CPI to stay above 2% at least for the year ahead, with the recent rise in wage momentum that should lift services inflation. This realization of the long-awaited virtuous cycle likely will lead the BoJ to proceed with policy normalization at a gradual pace. The BoJ is currently waiting for further indications of a trend rise in wages as well as its pass though to inflation, particularly for services (Figures 5 and 6).

Figure 5: Japan core prices (ex. all food and energy)
![](images/1f2b3c3666dc0a4a95fe23539a314562fcb5f082977a72d50a4e7045c4f73bf9.jpg)

Figure 6: Japan Services prices
![](images/a698e7a05953306d9489e3f5f25923f98d2d7d8c82821390c13ff828fae7f696.jpg)
Source: Statistics Bureau, J.P. Morgan

The official core CPI (ex. only fresh food, de-facto headline) decelerated from 2.5%oya in November to 2.3% in December, due to the decline in electricity prices, which reflected lagged effects of lower imported fuel prices with around a six-month lag. Looking ahead, however, the trend of weak energy prices will likely reverse starting from the February print, reflecting the rise in underlying electricity prices, as well as the end of the government<sup>’</sup>s energy subsidy programs and its positive base effects.

In the January Tokyo CPI report, we expect the global core CPI (ex. food and energy) annual rate of inflation to remain unchanged at 2.7%, as stronger services inflation likely will be offset by a trend declining core goods prices. We expect the official core CPI (ex. only fresh food, de-facto headline)

to remain close to the BoJ target of 2% until the January print, due to a decline in electricity prices, which reflects lagged effects of lower imported fuel prices. But we expect this to rise again in February, with a rise in electricity prices starting from the month.

## Review of past week’s data

## Canada

• We expect the Bank of Canada to hold the policy rate next week

• We continue to look for a first cut in 2Q24

Real construction spending continued to build during 2H23

Next week we expect the Bank of Canada to remain on hold. Since we anticipate no change in the policy rate, the spotlight will be on what it says and not on what it does. Our focus will be on updated growth and inflation forecasts, any adjustment to the forward guidance, and the language in the press conference. Recent comments have pushed back against an early pivot from the Bank<sup>’</sup>s current hawkish guidance. Considering the current inflation backdrop it<sup>’</sup>s hard to see the Bank using the January meeting to change the guidance and signal that it intends to ease policy at the subsequent meeting in March. The Bank of Canada<sup>’</sup>s core inflation measures have been stubbornly above the Bank<sup>’</sup>s target range through most of 2023. Measured on a three-month annualized basis, core inflation has been stuck in a range of 3.0% to 4.0% for a year. While the trend slipped within the target range in November, it hooked back up in December. The underlying inflation data do not support the view that inflation is on a sustainable downward path toward the 2.0% target hence we don<sup>’</sup>t think the Bank of Canada is in any rush to cut the policy rate.

Figure 1: Bank of Canada core CPIs
![](images/27846128614a3ce8669609a694369ede34e8ccbaf23a26b134140e8c7e59aee9.jpg)

We expect the first cut in 2Q23; by then the Bank will have a much better sense of the downward trend in inflation and wage growth that we anticipate. Some important aspects of the 4Q Business Outlook Survey point to continued normalization. The survey showed that inflation expectations continued to ease, albeit gradually, and both expected corporate pricing behavior and expected wage growth point to more normalization. More firms are reporting that the size of their price changes will continue to moderate over the next 12 months on the back of weaker demand and heightened competition and average expected wage increases continue to trend downward.

Figure 2: BOS: Average expected wage increase %
![](images/24f7393a2bd487c96143b2f538b67c0050fd49f4a41f39d1f016bf9df1bb45f5.jpg)

The December consumer price index (CPI) increased 0.3%sa as we expected, lifting the year-ago increase to 3.4% from 3.1% in November. The core CPI (excluding food and energy) eased to 0.2%sa and the year-ago gain in that measure ticked down to 3.4%. Core goods prices picked up to 2.2%oya, the firmest pace since September while core services prices dropped 3 ticks to 4.0%oya.

## Cooler weather, changing leaves, shorter days, and construction

Real construction investment turned the corner at the start of the second half of 2023 after 12 straight months of decline. Real investment in new construction has shown impressive gains through November with activity up almost 10% since July. The gains extend to both residential and nonresidential activity. It<sup>’</sup>s likely that sentiment in the construction sector may have been negatively impacted by climbing interest rates and historic wildfire conditions nationally in 1H23.The rebound in construction investment points to the waning effects of higher interest rates as monetary policy has been on hold since last July. Moreover to encourage the construction of more housing across the country, the federal government has committed C\$4 billion in the form of low interest loans to fund to new projects. We estimate real construction contributed as much as 1.0%-pt to growth in 4Q23. The evolving data flow continues to support our better-than-consensus forecasted pace of 1.5%ar real GDP growth in 4Q23.

## Mexico

• Early 2024 inflation to reflect some renewed food-related pressures and slightly stronger services

We expect 1H Jan CPI at 4.83%oya (0.43%m/m); core below 5% (0.41%) for the first time since 3Q21

• Economic activity for 4Q to remain mixed with a modest downside risk to our 2.5%saar

• Pre-campaigns ended with incumbent Morena as front-runner; constitutional reforms in focus

Next week<sup>’</sup>s preliminary January CPI print should hammer home the same message as in recent reports: disinflation is becoming slower and tougher. In fact, annual headline inflation has recently reaccelerated, and we expect it to remain broadly unchanged at 4.83%oya (0.43%2w/2w); core should print at 0.41%2w/2w and close to 5%oya. The underlying picture remains mostly unchanged, with sequential core price gains still at a level that is not consistent with the convergence of inflation to the central bank<sup>’</sup>s target range.

Factoring our preliminary forecast into our estimate for the month, we would expect core CPI to increase 0.4%m/m, sa (Figure 1) and about 4.7% on a 3m/3m annualized basis.

![](images/521e0b9d916813ace8b26490a92ae6822a787019f474eb3719b7dfd57e000652.jpg)

This time around we don<sup>’</sup>t expect goods inflation to be as benign as in past months, as we look for a pickup in nonfood goods. Additionally, services should see their pace of increase pick back up to around 0.5%m/m, sa largely on account of services excluding housing and education, the prices of which we forecast preliminarily at 0.6%m/m, sa in January. We expect services inflation to remain troublesome amid an economy working above its capacity (Figure 2).

There are a couple of risks to our inflation outlook—one that does not see the 3m/3m annualized run rate of inflation below 4% in 1H: to the upside, that core goods normalization has run its course and will no longer help contain large gains in services prices; to the downside, that growth is weaker than we expect during the first half of the year.

![](images/34459c527f1329e136650ca01ccf888048d742c47a59fc5f5df26dc1cd2077c2.jpg)

## Services to offset weak Nov IP

This week<sup>’</sup>s November activity data were on average a bit weaker than we expected, as retail sales rose less than we expected (0.1%m/m vs 0.7%) but service-sector reported income rose a solid 0.9% in the month. This leaves us comfortable with our earlier guidance on a broad basis: services output should pose a solid gain and help offset weakness in industrial production, which surprisingly tumbled in the middle month of last quarter.

We look for services to have risen 0.5%m/m (Figure 3), and the overall GDP proxy to have remained flat in November. This implies we<sup>’</sup>d need to see a strong gain in December data for our 2.5%ar GDP forecast to remain viable.

Figure 3: November GDP proxy
![](images/61b1440b7e2d294f26ad4aa9bcef556d04aba1043b0563b3e22a39af8416711c.jpg)
Source: INEGI and J.P. Morgan forecasts

We think there are reasons to expect such strength: services survey data showed a large gain in reported income and perceived demand in December while manufacturing should be less of a drag (we expect manufacturing exports to have eked out a 0.3%m/m gain in December) and construction should bounce back in the month—also judging by survey data. The risks seem tilted to the downside, though not meaningfully so.

From a demand-side perspective, there has been no considerable shift in underlying fundamentals and thus the case for a strong consumer and rising investment remains in place—we expect December import data to be consistent with this outlook for domestic demand.

## Comfortable lead for Morena at the end of pre-campaigns

As we reach the end of the pre-campaigns stage, the incumbent Morena party and its presidential candidate Ms. Sheinbaum maintain a comfortable lead ahead of the June 2 federal elections, with recent polls confirming a wider advantage over the center-right opposition alliance FAM and its candidate Ms. Xóchitl Gálvez (Figure 4). Movimiento Ciudadano (MC), the social democracy party, is well behind with Alvarez Maynez holding approximately 7% of the voter preference.

Figure 4: Voting preference
![](images/0cf1284e9d846235542d597e5ad58d6e342a804376fdde53c8ed64edce1a2bb6.jpg)
Source: El Financiero poll as of January 4th 2024

At a local level, a recently released poll from the El Heraldo newspaper indicated that Morena is ahead in seven out of the nine states scheduled to hold gubernatorial elections. Of note, in the two states where it trails the FAM bloc, Morena is in a close race, as opposed to the comfortable advantage in the other seven. If polls are correct, Morena is on track to govern 75% of the country.

Regarding voting intentions for Congress, Morena is still expected to win a simple majority. The incumbent<sup>’</sup>s ultimate goal is to secure a two-thirds majority in both legislative chambers, which would allow it to pass additional constitutional reforms. Although Morena<sup>’</sup>s lead is substantial, it is still 5% to 10%-pts below a constitutional majority; however with Sheinbaum aided by a strong economic momentum and Galvez lacking a consistent strategy from a weak coalition, we believe the possibility of a qualified majority is not negligible.

Still, this majority probably will require support from the coalition parties (far-right and far-left) in addition to MC. Constitutional changes proposed by President Lopez Obrador (AMLO) earlier this month suggest broader support from smaller groups is not far-fetched and that they could be discussed even ahead of the elections, in the next ordinary session of Congress (February - April).

The president announced last week that he would be sending to Congress, on February 5, two constitutional reforms on

wages and the pension system; while these could be delayed until the first session under the new Congress in September, the announcement of such reforms will likely be a shot in the arm for the incumbent<sup>’</sup>s campaign.

## Brazil

• November economic activity data were stronger than expected

• Services output, retail sales, and IP grew in the month

• But GDP growth probably remained weak in 4Q23 on some payback from agriculture

• Strength of economic growth in 2024 should help to preclude BCB from rushing the easing pace

The economic activity data for November were the most upbeat in months. For the first time in a while, all three main monthly economic indicators grew on a sequential basis, with all of them—services, retail sales and industrial production— surprising to the upside. Though driven by the mining sector, IP grew 0.5% in November with retail sales boosted by Black Friday rising 1.3% and accelerating to 2.6%3m/3m, saar, the strongest performance of the second half of the year. And this was not a goods-only story: after a period of significant and widespread weakness between August and October, services output sent better signals in November, with services associated with household consumption a highlight (Figure 1).

![](images/a83ef515eaf2d5f8c37e135745cc8a329feace44357b50aa437b1068c89c4011.jpg)

It is too soon to extrapolate November<sup>’</sup>s performance into the next months and quarters. In fact, some payback in agriculture—a sector that helped to boost growth in the first half of 2023—likely continued depressing economic performance in the second half of last year.

Overall, while the nowcaster suggests a small upside risk to our forecast for stability in GDP growth in 4Q (Figure 2), the recent data are aligned with our expectations of a weak 4Q followed by growth accelerating in early 2024 and then sustaining a near 2%ar pace. In our view, with the strength of the aggregate balance sheet of households, companies, and banks, the improvements in supply-side conditions such as in the oil sector, and the impulse (albeit limited) from the precatórios payment, growth should remain resilient ahead.

Figure 2: Brazil 4Q nowcaster
![](images/ec7ae832b9a9346febfc3644e8995bcf2e3f6e929463cec8fb5901c178adf1b0.jpg)
Source: J.P. Morgan

If we are correct, the strength of economic performance this year should help to preclude BCB from accelerating the pace of cuts. We then don<sup>’</sup>t see the recent data interfering with this month<sup>’</sup>s COPOM decision, or with the message of keeping the same pace at the following meetings. The combination of a core CPI run rate close to the target and GDP growth likely keeping a healthy pace during 2024 appears aligned with a pace of 50bp cuts ahead as there is still ample space to keep reducing the tightness of monetary policy before the SELIC rate reaches its nominal neutral, estimated at 8%-8.5%.

## Argentina

• High frequency inflation data point to headline infla tion at 20%m/m in January, in line with our forecast

• We keep Dec 24 inflation forecast unchanged at 210%oya (320%oya average inflation)

The BCRA has purchased US\$4.8mn in reserves since taking office (US\$1.9bn YTD)

But the ability to accumulate reserves depends on the interaction between the official REER and the FX gap

• If our projected inflation profile pans out, a new recalibration of the official FX would be required

## Inflation still runs hot

One characteristic of stabilization efforts is that a necessary condition for inflation to eventually converge lower is the prior realignment of relative prices, including the exchange rate, while eradicating administered prices and controls. In Argentina, given the accumulation of controls and policy-induced disarray of relative prices in prior years, it should come as no surprise that inflation is to first accelerate, to open a path of convergence to the rest of the world average levels in the medium to long term. Admittedly, such convergence cannot be attained if not underpinned by a sustained fiscal effort.

Regarding inflation, after leaping higher to 25.5%m/m in December, the pace in early 2024 has moderated a bit, in line with our forecast, though remains fairly elevated. For the second week of January, the high frequency inflation data published by EcoGo show headline inflation tracking in line with our forecast for the month at 20%m/m, a deceleration from the prior month but still the highest since February 1991.

In a more granular analysis, food CPI showed an acceleration from prior week, at 4.8%w/w, from 3.1%w/w in the first week of the month. Therefore, food CPI has accumulated a 16.3%m/m rise through mid-January, when incorporating the high 12.3% statistical carryover from December. Assuming food CPI inflation decelerates to a weekly average of 4% in 2HJan, we project food CPI at 21.3% for the full month.

Details matter. Breaking down by components, core CPI is running at a still high 22.1%m/m, while our underlying metric (core ex. food) tracks at 24.6% through mid-January. Meanwhile, regulated and seasonal prices are still running well below headline level, at 13.9% and 14.6%m/m, respectively. This is of relevance, as regulated prices are poised to continue increasing in the coming months given the need to trim subsidies, and realign relative prices. As an example, fuels have already increased by another 27% in January, and increases in prepaid medical services, public transport, and communication services are also expected this month. On utility prices, the government is to hold public hearings to define energy tariff increases ahead, likely coming into effect next month.

In all, as we flagged recently, while we expect deceleration ahead from December<sup>’</sup>s monthly peak, inflation is still likely to remain elevated in 1H24 amid the need to correct the sizable relative prices distortion swiftly to support stabilization.

In our base case of a successful program, inflation is expected to print at around 17%m/m on average during 1H24, decelerating to a monthly average of 4.7% in the second half of the year. That would be consistent with 2024 inflation at 210%oya by December 2024 (320%oya average inflation, Figure 1). Admittedly, the risks to our 2024 forecast are skewed to the upside given the sizable imbalances in place and implementation risks around the stabilization program.

![](images/9fe572b5e4541dc6ea21ed1106e2e7c91c3ec53fdc8a3e5d47466a9ac35e73e8.jpg)

## Reserves accumulation and the FX gap

Beyond inflation momentum, other two key variables to track against the backdrop of the negotiations in Congress for the omnibus bill are the pace of international reserves accumula tion and the official-parallel FX gap. The BCRA has purchased US\$4.8bn in reserves since taking office (US\$1.9bn YTD), benefiting from the year-end official FX adjustment higher, wheat exports, and prevailing limits on imports payments (Figure 2). Ahead, the agreement with the IMF set a reserve accumulation target for US\$7.3bn for the year.

Figure 2: Reserves accumulation and FX gap US\$mn lhs, % rhs
![](images/e5d6744ab54504508d70a2666e229f92fbfc5963cd8e8f5ef0446d192e817ef2.jpg)

In our estimates, February and March will be months of limited ability to purchase net reserves, while the expected record harvest should alleviate the tensions in 2Q24. The magnitude of such respite, however, depends on the interaction between the official FX REER and the FX gap.

As discussed above, December<sup>’</sup>s CPI print is likely to prove a monthly peak but our inflation estimates for 1H24 are still high amid the need to correct the relative prices distortion, suggesting a quick appreciation of the real exchange rate. If our projected inflation profile pans out, a new re-calibration of the official FX would be required to secure the ability to accumulate reserves in 2H24, and thus phase out capital controls this year. Regarding the FX gap, despite the IMF Staff agreement, the gap has continued to print above 50%.

The author wishes to thank Juan Goldin, of the Latin America Economics Research team, J.P.Morgan Chase Bank Sucursal Buenos Aires, for his contribution to this report.

## Andeans

Colombia: The monthly activity indicator showed a stronger-than-expected performance in November

The output gap continues to narrow and should be close to null if not negative already

2024 GDP revised higher to a still low 1.2%y/y on less of negative carryover and El Niño risks moderating

Peru: November activity print aligns with our base case for growth to continue firming ahead

2024 GDP forecast unchanged, but risk is now skewed to the upside

## Colombia: Stronger-than-expected activity

The monthly activity index reported by DANE showed a stronger-than-expected activity performance in November.

Activity recovered in November, printing above our expectations at 0.9%m/m, sa (JPM +0.4%). The November bounce back thus basically erases October<sup>’</sup>s 1.1% drop (revised from -1.0%). Thus, despite the strong print in November, 3m/3m sequential activity remained in the red, at -1.6%saar. On an annual basis, activity gained 2.3%oya (nsa), and, following series revisions, +1.0% YTD.

The main surprise in the month was primary production, which gained 2.5%m/m, sa. Primary sectors are thus growing at a 7.8%3m/3m, saar pace, showing an upward trend since late 2022. On its side, secondary production also recovered on the month, up by 1.6%m/m, sa. Despite the gain, it fell short of offsetting October<sup>’</sup>s 2.3% plunge, continuing its downward trend. Finally, services slipped by a whisker on the month, down 0.03%m/m, sa, on the heels of a 0.2% slip in October.

On a trend basis, activity remains below the levels associated with pre-COVID trend growth, and the output gap continues to narrow and should be close to null if not negative already (Figure 1).

In terms of forecasts, we fine-tune 4Q23 GDP to less of a sequential contraction, and now we pencil in -1.6% ar (-2.2%, previously). The upward revision to the sa level is nonetheless associated with a downward revision of the 2023 annual GDP, explained by series revisions to nsa levels. Thus, we project 2023 GDP a tenth lower, at +1.1%y/y. Less of a negative statistical carryover and the perception that El Niño may not prove as dire for primary production as we had entertained prompts us to revise up 2024 GDP, to a still-low +1.2%y/y. That is consistent with 4Q24/4Q23 at +2.7%oya, close to its potential.

Figure 1: Colombia economic activity tracker Index, sa, 2015 =100
![](images/e391f56469def027dcd0b1d2f230735fdf06e4e46548b215db5676623d3de672.jpg)

## Peru: Activity growth firmed in November

When adjusting for seasonality, November activity gained 0.8 %m/m, sa, a stronger pace than we had forecasted (+0.5%). Therefore, the sequential momentum converged to 1.8 %3m/3m, saar, which compares to -0.4% in 3Q23. On an over-year-ago basis, activity recorded 0.3 %oya, and- 0.6%y/ y YTD.

The supply side breakdown is showing a wider distribution than in prior months, which aligns with our base case for growth to continue firming ahead. Mining, manufacturing, construction and agriculture expanded in November, a combination of primary and secondary performance that appears to be a necessary condition for sustained services gains ahead.

All in, by November <sup>’</sup>23 headline activity printed 3.1% above pre-pandemic levels as of February 2020, which implies a level 11.7% below that consistent with pre-COVID trend growth. Translating the subpar aggregated growth figures into a per capita basis, growth in the last three years printed 1.3 % below the levels as of February 2020. Note that annual per capita growth was +4.4%, on average, in the period 2004-19 (Figure 2).

Figure 2: Peru real activity, level and per capita Index, Dec 2018=100
![](images/6a6bbf721c4e44cfa53fe102801f01dc4f1c4fa380a880ebe18909f0f3b44221.jpg)

Ahead, monetary easing should allow some sectors to regain positive momentum, such as construction and some services. On the other side, a main risk to activity ahead comes from adverse weather shocks associated with El Niño, particularly by the end of Q1 and Q2. When it comes to forecasts, we revise 4Q23 sequential GDP a tad higher, to 3.6%ar, still consistent with annual GDP of -0.5%y/y. That would set a statistical carry-over of around one point of GDP when it comes to 2024 GDP. We maintain this year<sup>’</sup>s average annual GDP at +2.1%y/y, though note that forecast risk is now skewed higher, on both higher carry-over and El Niño risks moderating toward expectations for a moderate rather than strong event.

## United Kingdom

• Labour market still holding up, wages rebound

• Upside surprise to inflation in December, but headline to drop below 2% in Apr

• BoE to resist 1H24 easing, but we now look for an earlier cut in Aug

Retail sales drop in Dec, but strong real incomes to boost 2024 growth

This week we made some changes to our forecasts, modestly revising up our 2024 growth forecast by a cumulative 0.4%-pts, reflecting stronger real incomes and easier financial conditions (Table 1). Along with a more positive growth outlook, firmer prints on wages and the CPI this week should reduce the urgency for BoE to cut rates as early as 1H24. Nevertheless, with inflation still set to undershoot the BoE<sup>’</sup>s forecasts by a significant margin this year, we expect it will begin easing earlier than we previously expected (August instead of November).

## Monthly rebound in average earnings

The ONS again published a reduced scope labour market report this month, using experimental estimates for its labour force survey due to low response rates. The ONS had previously indicated it would be able to produce the full report for this release, incorporating new population estimates, but instead issued a statement last Friday saying it needed more time. As such, the picture on the labour market remains partial at this stage, notably with regards to how much the unemployment rate has risen over the past year.

On the basis of the experimental data, the ONS judged that the unemployment rate remained stable at 4.2%. This in turn is based on data that show the claimant count unemployment rate steady. And in the absence of data from its household employment survey, the ONS is relying on payroll data that continue to show employment broadly stable. The latest data show a decline of 24k in December, but the prior decline was revised to show a gain, and this measure of job growth is running at +0.8% on a 3m/3m annualised basis.

The vacancy data have been unaffected. This measure of unfilled job demand continued to decline in November, but the pace of decline is slowing. The vacancy rate remained at 3.0% for the third consecutive month; a large fall over the past year but historically still very high. Given the problems with the unemployment data - and the prospect of potential downward revisions in the spring - it remains difficult to say where the V/U is at the moment. And if growth were to do a little better early this year, this could fully stabilise vacancies at a high level.

The average earnings data have also been unaffected. In November there was a strong rebound of 0.6%m/m in private sector regular pay, but following a rare drop in the prior month (revised down from -0.2% to -0.4%) (Figure 1). The smoothed annual change in private sector regular average earnings (currently 6.5%) is on track to finish 2023 running closer to 6%, which would be more than a full percentage point below the BoE<sup>’</sup>s 7.2% forecast for 4Q. This should remove some of the upside risk around the BoE<sup>’</sup>s inflation forecast.

Figure 1: Average weekly earnings: private sector
%, excl. bonuses, all rates of change are annualized & seasonally adjusted
![](images/d64a0e45a37a71e8575c8b3574fc834252b84ee64b518297e934e609244be19d.jpg)

The 3m/3m annualised rate has also now fallen to 2.5%. This is a significant slowing from the 10% peak seen last summer, but the rebound in the latest month hints that the underlying run rate will settle higher than this. Two measures of wages (xPertHR pay settlements, and DMP business year ahead expectations) recently accelerated from rates already above 5%, which is too high to meet the inflation target. This would still imply upside risk around the BoE<sup>’</sup>s 4.25% forecast for 2024.

There is a much weaker forward-looking message about pay being sent by the REC (closer to 3%). However, the PAYEbased measure of pay that has caught attention of late because it also looked very weak (the mean slipped below 1%oya in November) produced a strong 1.5%m/m gain in the latest December reading. Amid these mixed signals, the BoE is likely to put weight on its own survey of 2024 pay expecta-

%3m, saar

tions at the upcoming February MPR. Early hints from the BoE point to a slowing below 6%, but that leaves a wide spectrum of possibilities. Our sense is that pay expectations will come in at around 5%, and this will make the MPC hesitant to cut rates early.

## Inflation surprises on the high side in Dec

The headline CPI rose 0.5%m/m in December, based on our seasonal adjustment, unexpectedly lifting the year-ago rate from 3.9% to 4.0%oya (J.P.Morgan and Consensus 3.8; BoE 4.6). Core prices also rose 0.5%m/m, sa to leave the year-ago rate steady at 5.1% (J.P.Morgan and Consensus 4.9%). Services inflation, under close scrutiny from the BoE at present, showed the biggest upside surprise, with a 0.7% m/m, sa gain to take the year-ago rate up from 6.3% to 6.4%oya (J.P.Morgan 6.0; Consensus 6.1; BoE 6.9) (Figure 2). This measure is nevertheless still far below the BoE<sup>’</sup>s November forecast. RPI inflation slowed from 5.3% to 5.2%oya (J.P.Morgan 5.0; Consensus 5.1).

Figure 2: Core inflation
![](images/4aa4b1a1cae2e2823d458a226365c49321af1a89df86c1753714cfb6226a1754.jpg)
Source: ONS, J.P. Morgan

The details show a 6.2%m/m, sa drop in liquid fuels as petrol prices fell, and another 0.4% decline in food and non-alcoholic beverages. Core goods prices rose 0.1%m/m, sa after two consecutive declines and perhaps will continue to stabilize, in part due to a rise in global supply chain pricing pressures owing to conflict in the Middle East.

Services prices were up 0.7%m/m, sa on our estimates, a very strong gain (the 2H23 average was 0.4%). However, the gain was relatively narrow and relates to large increases in transport services (sensitive to energy and seasonal patterns) package holidays and accommodation, and services for transport equipment. The MPC in December argued in favour of setting some of these components to one side as a better read on inflation persistence. Doing so would leave the December services m/m print looking a little weaker. However, on our initial estimate this exclusion-based measure would still be running at a firm 5.1% 3m/3m annualized pace (compared to “headline<sup>”</sup> services at 4.5%) due to strength in past months (Table 2). The MPC seems to be talking about this exclusion based measure of services in over a year ago terms and, on this basis, there is been a fairly gradual slowing from the peak last July.

Table 2: UK Services inflation
Adj. services excl. non-private rents, accommodation and airfares

Adj. 0.8 0.4</td><td>9.2 8.0</td><td>Unadj. 7.4</td><td>Adj. 7.0</td></tr><tr><td>Aug-23</td><td>0.1</td><td>7.8 6.7</td><td>6.8</td><td>6.7</td></tr><tr><td>Sep-23 0.6</td><td>0.4</td><td>6.7 5.7</td><td>6.9</td><td>6.8</td></tr><tr><td>Oct-23</td><td>0.5</td><td>5.1 5.5</td><td>6.6</td><td>6.8</td></tr><tr><td>0.3</td><td>0.5</td><td>4.7 5.4</td><td></td><td></td></tr><tr><td>Nov-23 0.2</td><td>0.3</td><td></td><td>6.3</td><td>6.4</td></tr><tr><td>Dec-23</td><td>0.7 0.4</td><td>4.5 5.1</td><td>6.4</td><td>6.3</td></tr></table>

Source: ONS, J.P. Morgan

PPI manufacturing prices dropped 0.6% over the month, while the ONS quarterly services PPI release showed a soft 0.4%q/q gain to leave the year ago rate high at 3.7%. Whether this falls much further is likely to be dependent on the path of wages this year, which again places close scrutiny on incoming early pay awards for this year (some hints are due next week). We have argued that the MPC would need to see pay for 2024 drop to around 4% to cut in 1H24.

Last week, we updated our inflation forecast to fully incorporate the November CPI surprise as well as our latest assumption that regulated domestic energy bills will fall by around 15% in April. These two factors shaved more than a percentage point off our forecast and left headline close to 1.5% during 2Q. This would be significantly below the BoE<sup>’</sup>s 3.6% forecast, which assumed a lot more persistence. After this December release, we think the 2Q trough in headline is still on track to come in around the same ballpark, at 1.6%. The surprise in headline was small in December, and some will likely be given back as seasonal factors push airfares back down again in January.

Despite the upside surprise in this December release, we are bringing forward our call for the first BoE hike from November to August (25bps). The case for a move around the autumn had been building, following the large downside inflation surprise from last month, but this week<sup>’</sup>s December print was important to see. Our inflation profile remains lower than before these two releases put together. And the latest profile, overall, is significantly below the MPC<sup>’</sup>s November forecasts.

Although the MPC has argued that the exclusion-based measure of services described above is more relevant, we think it will nevertheless show some sensitivity to the much lower paths of headline and overall core inflation now shown in our %q/q, saar for consumption, %3m/3m, saar for retail sales (ex. auto-fuel)

latest forecast, as well as the global narrative in which the odds of early easing from other central banks have risen significantly (notwithstanding this week<sup>’</sup>s moves). Nevertheless, we continue to push back on an easing as early as 1H24 and, for now, have resisted putting a 50bp cut for 3Q due to the upside surprises in this week<sup>’</sup>s CPI and wage data.

The change in our forecast largely brings forward more of the existing easing we had for 2025 and, hence, we haven<sup>’</sup>t changed our end-2025 forecast of 3%. Our new forecast has a cumulative 75bps of cuts for this year (25bp in 3Q and 50bp in 4Q). Previously this was 50bps for 4Q.

## Retail sales sales fell sharply in December

Retail sales showed a surprising and broad-based 3.2%m/m drop in volumes in December, reigniting jitters about growth after recent hints of improvement in other indicators.

While the ONS highlights that the data adjust for seasonal factors such as the inclusion of Cyber Monday (on this occasion) and the tendency to spend more in the run-up to Christmas, its seasonal adjustment does not account for the tendency to do Christmas shopping earlier in the year (which it says there was evidence of this year). As such, the December print at least needs to be taken together with the strong 1.4%m/m gain in November. Still, sales are down 0.9% over the quarter (-3.5% annualised) and the momentum into January is weak.

The direct contribution of retail output to GDP implies this will shave 0.04% off quarterly GDP growth in 4Q (0.2% annualised) as confirmed by the ONS. This may seem small, but owes to the retail sector contributing only 5% to gross value added. Note GDP is initially calculated from the output side in the UK.

A bigger concern for us is whether the retail sales drop is acting as a signal about overall consumer trends. Consumer spending accounts for 60% of GDP, and hence a large swing downward would have a much broader reach across many other sectors by output.

Figure 3 shows the relationship between retail sales and consumption in the national accounts, and illustrates that the correlation can be episodic at best. If the retail sales drop is signalling something meaningful in consumer behaviour, and the broader economy, we may expect sentiment to deteriorate and other activity indicators to fall too. Next week<sup>’</sup>s Gfk consumer confidence and flash PMI data will be important to gauge activity into January, but in their December readings, both showed a slight improvement over the month and hence the drop in retail sales should be treated with caution.

Figure 3: Consumption versus retail sales
![](images/36cd4b9e3329cc6285d22093798262757e059208331c618aab3838bc340acd53.jpg)

One related issue will be whether, and if so by how much, retail sales recover in January. While the impact on GDP for 4Q overall may be contained, the December GDP print will be affected more significantly. This will create negative momentum that spills over into the 1Q data, dragging down on growth in that quarter absent a clear rebound in January.

## Emerging Europe

• Israel: Another downside surprise in CPI

## • NBH: Guidance suggests frontloaded easing

Israel<sup>’</sup>s consumer price inflation brought another dovish surprise, slowing to 3.0%oya in December, the upper bound of the BoI<sup>’</sup>s target corridor (JPMe and consensus 3.1%). Details of the report were quite soft, too, as momentum in core remained very low. Relative to our expectations, food and energy printed higher, but none are of big concern to us in the near term, as dynamics in international prices remain benign. That said, momentum in both core goods (-2.0%m/m, saar) and core services (0.8%) surprised us to the downside again. By now it is clear that the demand shock dominated over-supply shocks in 4Q, while ILS strength further dampened momentum in December.

Although there were a couple of unusually large price declines, like recreational services or car rentals past month, which should ultimately reverse, they were not prime drivers of the softness in core. Heavy-weight categories like housing, furniture, clothing and communications recorded price declines, too. And, in general, the left tail of the distribution was fairly fat in December, i.e. price declines were widespread. The tail should get thinner with time, yet that the median has declined notably in recent months (0.1%m/m, saar, in December) suggests that the underlying price momentum has weakened substantially (Figure 1).

Figure 1: Israel CPI ex. food and energy vs. median inflation
![](images/74ea671e3db1a5b1a802dc26fad1a534918353336a7271bad9c7574110eee494.jpg)

Our key near-term inflation concern is around the effects of shipping disruptions on price dynamics. Israel faced a large drop in port calls and imports from November and the situation may have further deteriorated recently. Although much of the early declines in imports were likely related to lower demand, the recent weakness was more likely to have been associated with supply constraints, as demand has recovered strongly. We don<sup>’</sup>t think this will have large-scale or lasting consequences for CPI, but near-term momentum in goods may increase, nonetheless. The adjustment in ILS after it, arguably, overshot (got too strong) but if the ILS stabilizes

not too far from pre-war levels, it should affect neither CPI nor BoI decision-making, we believe. Further down the road, fiscal loosening appears to be the main risk.

Even with these risks in mind, our base-case scenario is for inflation to stay benign in the coming months, keeping the door for further policy easing open. We forecast inflation to drop close to the middle of the BoI<sup>’</sup>s target corridor by 2Q, with inflation expectations gradually sliding, too. Hence, the BoI will need to continue lowering the policy rate to avoid the real rate getting too restrictive when domestic demand has not yet fully recovered. We continue to expect the BoI to cut 25bp at the February meeting, seeing the policy rate at 3.75% by year-end.

## NBH: Room to frontload cuts

This week, Deputy Governor Virag signalled in public remarks that the NBH is open to a more frontloaded, rate-cutting cycle. Specifically, Virag said that the central bank would consider cuts of 75bps or 100bps in the next decision (Jan 30), but argued that the case for 100bps is now stronger than it was in December, with inflation likely to come close to the upper end of the NBH<sup>’</sup>s 2-4%oya target range in spring. This would imply an acceleration from the 75bp/meeting pace favoured by the NBH after the simplification of the monetary policy framework last October, Virag also said that if the NBH speeds the pace to 100bps, it will do so only for one to three months and that market pricing of 6-7% rates by midyear appeared <sup>”</sup>realistic<sup>”</sup>.

Hungary<sup>’</sup>s inflation has come a long way, having fallen from 25% at the start of last year to 5.5%oya in December, which quite remarkably is now the lowest and closest to target in the region (Figure 2). From that perspective, double-digit rates are excessive and there is room for some room for frontloading rate cuts. However, given the volatile history of monetary policy in Hungary, we think any frontloading of the easing cycle should be communicated purely as that, avoiding expectations of a deeper easing cycle from settling in.

Unlike headline inflation, core CPI in in Hungary is the highest in the region and the latest data points raise question marks over how low CPI can settle. Although core disinflation was supported by both goods and services, there was for a long time an over-reliance on core goods, where the disinflationary impulse has faded. Core services have stagnated for half a year around the 6-8% pace and with wage growth in double digits are unlikely to slow significantly more in 2024. We expect core inflation to be at 4.7%oya at the end of 2024, whereas our preferred measure, core CPI ex all food, is likely to fall to 5.3%oya.

Figure 2: CEE inflation versus central bank targets
![](images/7762f1eafb2b5467ef624d7d3585ee3ea20193325bc711f83d672e0a2cf0c2cf.jpg)

Given the clear guidance form the central bank, we are now expecting 100bps cuts in both January and February (75bps previously), after which we see the NBH gradually reducing the pace of rate cuts. This frontloading of cuts does not change our overall view of the easing cycle, with the 5% terminal rate still reached in 1Q25, but one month earlier (Janu ary instead of February). Under this rate path, real rates will be only marginally positive at the end of the year and about zero if deflated by core CPI instead of headline. In our view, this is already a dovish stance: venturing much beyond here the central bank would have to be willing to accept a higher CPI path and weaker FX.

We expect the CBRT to hike its policy rate by 250bp to 45% as the CBRT signalled the tightening to be completed as soon as possible. Inflation outlook deteriorated in January due to higher than expected minimum wage hikes, tax hikes and administered price hikes. We expect hawkish forward guidance leaving the door open for further rate hikes if needed. We do not expect premature rate cuts this year, and maintain our end-2024 policy rate forecast (and terminal rate) at 45% (or higher) to reign in inflation.

Source: TUIK, CBRT, J.P. Morgan forecast

## South Africa

Inflation likely slowed in December, but core inflation is drifting up near term

• SARB to remain guarded and retain 8.25% policy rate

• Fiscal developments curb related FX-passthrough risks

Recent SARB communication has retained a focus on inflation dynamics and indirectly cautioned against expectations of early and deep rate cuts. We expect the upcoming MPC meeting to reiterate this message, although the softening of inflation risks suggests a more balanced tone. The December inflation report probably recorded a drop in headline inflation to 5.2%, from 5.5% in November as food and transport inflation eased (Figure 1). Transport costs benefitted from a gasoline price cut at the pump as well as base effects. Food price dynamics m/m remain volatile and there is upside risk to our projections of an easing to 8.4%oya (from 9% in November). We expect food inflation to soften further to 6% by year-end, with recent heavy rains trimming upside risks from El Niño.

Figure 1: South Africa inflation outlook
![](images/456d48acb2c3fa00911af72997852241d8d0fc82c80db6e8084493ea707a02a3.jpg)

A remaining risk is that of a near-term upward drift in core inflation, which we see ticking up 0.3%m/m to 4.6% in December. We expect the oya rate to rise further to 4.8% in February, from 4.5% in November. This is in part due to the shifting survey dates of health care, but also tied to likely rising rental and public transportation inflation. In December, the quarterly survey of OER and rents likely shows a pick up to 3.4%oya, from 2.6% previously, although previous surveys persistently surprised to the downside. The subsequent two inflation reports more likely see headline inflation temporarily rise again to 5.5%, before inflation hopefully more consistently settles around 5% through to 3Q24, before drifting lower to 4.5% in 4Q.

While market pricing suggests a chance of a repo rate cut as early as March, we continue to look for cuts to begin only in May. True, idiosyncratic risks have diminished somewhat, particularly as high-frequency fiscal data signal only limited revenue risks despite softening terms of trade, as reflected in better than expected government cash balances in December. Moreover, the possibility of an agreement on the drawdown of Treasury<sup>’</sup>s GFECRA balances at the SARB could further limit fiscal pressures and related FX-passthrough to inflation. Yet, the MPC likely will only act once a 4.5% inflation print is within imminent reach and adverse risks from food and commodity prices have subsided. We expect the SARB to retain most of their macro forecasts unchanged at this meeting, with inflation expected to average 5% (JPMe: 4.9%) this year and 4.5% in 2025, with growth improving to 1.2% in 2024 on the back of less severe supply side constraints. Similarly, the projected QPM-repo rate path probably is unchanged, projecting a policy rate of 7.55% by year-end.

## Australia and New Zealand

• Australia<sup>’</sup>s unemployment rate held at 3.9% in December 2023, in line with expectations

The number of employed fell, but we fade this as the ABS flagged some seasonal abnormalities

• We expect NZ 4Q23 headline CPI to come in at 0.4%q/q nsa ...

• … less than the RBNZ<sup>’</sup>s forecast (0.8%q/q)

Australian unemployment held steady at 3.9% sa (Figure 1) in December as we and consensus expected, but with substantial negative moves in employment, the part-time/full-time split, and participation. This comes after a couple of months of stronger outcomes on participation in particular, so we don't read a lot into the latter details, particularly with the ABS flagging some seasonal abnormalities in the monthly pattern through 4Q, and also that cyclones/flooding in QLD affected survey collections in the month. The latter can bias the finer details of the survey, though we have no reason doubt the aggregate unemployment outcome.

Figure 1: Australian unemployment rate
![](images/048d9e4758fdff695798fb17043e0c4a03e9d861ca2db6d135bdb4d3bf3af892.jpg)
Source: ABS, J.P. Morgan

The labor market has been behaving broadly as expected; leading indicators of demand (ads/vacancies/business surveys) have moderated, and this is flowing through to weaker average hours worked and an upward bias to unemployment over time, but the process still looks gradual and orderly.

For the RBA, this week's result is in line with the last SoMP projections for the 4Q average, which came after repeated upgrades (i.e. lowering of the unemployment path). So over the last year, the labor market has generally been more resilient than expected and in our view this will see the board carrying over December's still-watchful tone, with reluctance to pre-emptively endorse cuts in 2024, in contrast to the Fed.

## NZ inflation decelerating sharply

We expect NZ 4Q23 CPI will increase 0.4%q/q nsa in next week<sup>’</sup>s release. The final tracking inputs released earlier in the week were soft enough on net to crystallize the significant downside risk to the RBNZ's standing forecast of 0.8%q/q heading into next week's quarterly report. Food prices were down 0.1%m/m, taking the annual rate down to 4.8%oya, the lowest since late '21. Further falls are due given the sequentials (quarterly was negative through 4Q overall). This is a long-expected catch-up to the global food price dynamic, which NZ had lagged due to currency and domestic labor supply issues in the primary sector, both of which are now abating. Other monthly price components released this week (mostly airfares) provided a partial offset.

With all inputs in hand (comprising around 45% of the basket) there appears to be significant downside risk to the RBNZ's standing CPI forecast. This follows weakness in the last GDP report (level of GDP around 1% lower than expected after revisions) which should cascade through the output gap estimates, and the NZIER business survey this week, which showed labor supply/cost/wage issues fading fast.

## Greater China

• China: 4Q23 GDP in line with expectations as policy impact starts to feed through

• PBOC kept policy rate unchanged; trade-offs in China’s monetary policy decision

• Bloomberg reported China to issue 1 trillion yuan special sovereign bonds

• Taiwan: Election outcome in line with expectations

• Next week: Taiwan export orders and IP, Hong Kong CPI and trade

China<sup>’</sup>s 4Q GDP printed in line with expectations, growing 5.2% oya. Our seasonal adjustment process suggests that the economy grew 5.8% q/q saar in 4Q, following the expansion of 6.9% in 3Q. For full-year 2023, real GDP grew 5.2%, modestly higher than the official growth target of “around 5%.<sup>”</sup> In nominal terms, 4Q GDP rose 3.7%oya, compared to 3.5% in 3Q. The implied GDP deflator fell 1.4% oya in 4Q, unchanged from 3Q. In the breakdown, consumption contributed to 2023 GDP growth by 82.5% (or 4.3%pts), while investment contributed 28.9% (1.5%pts), and net exports dragged by 0.6%pt.

December activity data came in somewhat mixed, with industrial production in line with expectations, further weakness from retail sales and housing activity, along with a modest uptick in fixed investment trend. IP fell 0.8% m/m sa in December, partly paying back a 1.5% gain in November. Retail sales disappointed on a 0.2% m/m sa fall. Service production growth slowed down to 8.5%oya (vs. 9.3%oya in November). Fixed investment rose 4.0%oya in December, with an uptick in both manufacturing FAI and infrastructure FAI, likely reflecting the impact of recent policy support measures. Real estate FAI weakened further, with a 12.4% decline (Figure 1). Private investment remained sluggish, falling 0.4%oya for 2023.

Figure 1: FAI growth by industry
![](images/01c21d2959868b44f78085379277c82b8d368192b577531d2006567d6366e626.jpg)

Regarding labor market conditions, the nationwide urban survey-based unemployment rate edged up to 5.1% in December (vs. 5.0% in November). The NBS also announced new data on unemployment breakdown by age for December: The unemployment rate for 16- to 24-year-olds came in at 14.9%; for 25- to 29-year-olds at 6.1%; and for 30- to 59-year-olds at 3.9%.

Looking forward, we think the government will likely set the 2024 annual growth target unchanged at “around 5%.<sup>”</sup> While the government is unlikely to introduce a massive policy stimulus, the growth-friendly policy stance should continue into 2024, in our view, with strengthened coordination between fiscal and monetary policies. We expect the impact of the latest policy easing measures, as well as further upcoming policy support, to be reflected in the coming quarters. We expect the economy to exhibit stable recovery momentum in 1H24 (5.1%q/q saar) before moderating to trend growth in 2H24 (4.3%q/q saar) (Figure 2). Our full-year growth forecast for 2024 stands at 4.9%.

Figure 2: China real GDP growth
![](images/3dfb5778bc7564856bb73d3de693eb4f7e50f20745f4346f155e0a3617fff5cb.jpg)

## PBOC kept policy rate unchanged

The PBOC kept the 1Y MLF rate unchanged, at 2.5%, in contrast to market consensus. The size of MLF operation is 995bn yuan, implying a net injection of 216bn yuan. This is after a 600bn yuan net liquidity injection via MLF in November and another 800bn yuan in December. The PBOC<sup>’</sup>s MLF balance has expanded by 2.1 trillion yuan since September 2023, while it had stayed roughly unchanged between December 2020 and August 2023 (from 5.15 trillion yuan to 5.195 trillion yuan).

We are not surprised at the policy decision, as it reflects the trade-offs faced by the PBOC. Factors supporting rate cuts include continued deflation pressure, heavy debt repayment burdens, mitigated pressure from currency depreciation and capital outflows and a structural decline in the natural interest rate. However, policy constraints come from limited room for monetary easing, constraints in the banking sector (NIM compression and capital adequacy pressure) and a debate on policy effectiveness.

We believe the PBOC will stick to the so-called “attenuation approach,<sup>”</sup> which means the pace of rate cuts will be modest. We maintain our view of measured monetary easing, with two 10bp rate cuts (March and September) and two 25bp RRR cuts (2Q and 4Q) this year (Figure 3). An important change is expanded use of structural monetary policy instruments (PSL and re-lending).

Figure 3: China policy rates and RRR
![](images/1d3ee6b475220b7a994c71f4acdcacf80c301d37413553d06fb2f9e0ffe411a4.jpg)

## China likely to issue 1 trillion yuan special CGB

Bloomberg reported on 16 January that Chinese policymakers are considering issuing 1 trillion yuan of special sovereign bonds (equivalent to 0.8% of GDP) with an ultra-long repayment period. This report echoes the earlier onshore news from 21Jingji on 10 January that local governments are working on project pitches. Targeted areas likely include food, energy and supply chain security, new urbanization and rural development, etc., per Bloomberg.

This development would not be a major surprise to us. We think it would likely be a new 1 trillion yuan special CGB for 2024, not a reiteration of the 1 trillion yuan budget deficit lift in late 2023. If it were to follow the historical special sovereign bonds scheme, it would be under central government debt management, but not affect headline fiscal deficit. Our baseline forecast looks for a 2024 fiscal deficit at 3.8% of GDP. If this development were to come to pass, we think the government may announce a 3.0% official deficit plus a 1 trillion yuan special central government bond at the upcoming March NPC meeting.

Our baseline forecast looks for a 3.5 trillion yuan special LGB quota for 2024. If the government relaxes the criteria for qualified projects to include public housing, urban village renovation, etc. (a supplementary funding support to PSL), the March NPC meeting may set a higher-than-expected quota, likely close to 4 trillion yuan. If that happens, we think there will be stricter scrutiny of LGFV debt expansion. Taken together, we maintain our 2024 augmented fiscal deficit at

12.2% of GDP, 0.1% marginally higher than 2023.

## Hong Kong: U-rate unchanged at 2.9%

Hong Kong<sup>’</sup>s unemployment rate stayed at 2.9% sa for the three months ending December 2023. Total employment fell 1.9k, to 3,706.8k, while the labor force fell 8.6k, to 3,812.5k. Seasonally adjusted, total employment fell 0.1%m/m sa, while the labor force also fell 0.1%m/m sa. The underemployment rate remained at 1.0%.

The imbalance in cross-border travel activities continued in the first half of January 2024 and is likely to weigh on consumption and employment in tourism-related sectors in the near term. Nevertheless, the city<sup>’</sup>s unemployment rate has returned to the lowest level since the onset of the pandemic, and we expect it to remain around 3% in the near term.

## Taiwan: No surprise from election outcome

Mr. Lai Ching-Te from the traditionally pro-independence Democratic Progressive Party (DPP) (the ruling party since 2016) claimed victory in Taiwan<sup>’</sup>s presidential election on 13 January, obtaining 40.05% of the total votes. This is the first time in Taiwan<sup>’</sup>s election history that a ruling party has won a presidential election for a third term. For the Legislative Yuan election, out of the total 113 seats, the DPP won 51 seats, while the KMT won 52 seats and the TPP won 8 seats, indicating that no single party has a majority position in the Legislative Yuan.

The election is unlikely to have significant impact on Taiwan<sup>’</sup>s economic growth in 2024, and Mr. Lai will likely continue with the DPP government<sup>’</sup>s general policy stance. But there could be a near-term impact on the economic side, particularly cross-strait economic links. Exports to Mainland China/HK SAR as a share of Taiwan<sup>’</sup>s total exports stand at around about 36%, compared to 41% prior to the U.S.-China tariff war (Figure 4).

Figure 4: Taiwan exports to Mainland China/ HK % share of total exports, 12mma
![](images/f2c7457e01937ddbd29dd6223f1bd17d01d36b2652c7a8f0d4cf53f836cf7ff9.jpg)

During Taiwan<sup>’</sup>s latest election campaign, there was growing attention to the outlook for the Cross-Strait Economic Cooperation Framework Agreement (ECFA), which was signed in June 2010 and came into effect September 2010. About 16.8% of Taiwan<sup>’</sup>s total exports to Mainland China enjoyed tariff reductions under the ECFA early harvest list as of 2022 (covering non-tech sectors including petrochemicals, textile, machinery and equipment, agriculture, transportation equipment, etc.), with around US\$0.8 billion of annual tariff concessions on Taiwan exports to Mainland China. Potential adjustments to the ECFA arrangement by Beijing could challenge Taiwan<sup>’</sup>s non-tech exports to Mainland China.

## Korea

• Trend moderation in import price inflation continues

• Semiconductor export prices rose further to suggest terms of trade recovery

Despite recent geopolitical impacts on trade prices through shipping costs, this week<sup>’</sup>s data suggest that the terms of trade recovery continued last month. Recent crude oil price moves does not suggest a material change in the view on the nearterm imported inflation, while the semiconductor price recovery confirms that the tech cycle continues on the expected recovery phase.

## Import prices moderated in trend

Import prices in local currency terms rose 1.4%m/m, sa, in December, after falling 0.6% in October and moving sideways in November (Figure 1). They rose broadly across products last month, by 0.5% for energy and 1.6% for ex-energy; among non-commodities, import prices rose 1.8% for intermediate goods and 1.2% for finished goods. This broad-based price rise was primarily due to inflationary currency effects, as the trade-weighted KRW depreciated by 1.1% during the month. Excluding such effects, overall import prices in contractual currency terms fell 0.2% in December following 0.7% declines in the prior two months.

Figure 1: Import prices in local currency terms
![](images/973934e09250ade88c69cb8288ab9982f040153765877de7f86a8638dfe8d21c.jpg)
Source: BoK, and J.P. Morgan

Currency effects were less significant when the monthly volatilities averaged out, so sequentially, import prices (in local currency terms) reflected a moderation in external price conditions: Their inflation slowed again to 9.7%3m/3m, saar in December after increasing temporarily to an 18% pace in the prior two months, as global oil prices have recently settled down from 3Q23<sup>’</sup>s hump.

Going forward, we expect import price pressures to stabilize further, assuming stable global oil prices as a baseline. This should contribute to a gradual moderation in final consumer prices with a time lag: Goods CPI has been closely related to import prices after about 1-3 months, based on past observations (Figure 2), while services CPI was likely affected through a more indirect channel in a delayed manner.

Figure 2: Korea import prices and CPI
![](images/1790a48eed1876ba635b38a7dc76395ba6491cc61b495aafdd648d7d61fab2e7.jpg)
Source: BoK and J.P. Morgan

## Positive sign for terms of trade

Export prices in local currency terms rose 1.7%m/m, sa, in December, rising for four months in the past five by 1.4% on average. The terms of trade (TOT) ticked up in December; zooming out, TOT has moved sideways since it stopped a 2.5-year-long slide in September 2022, and its current level is only slightly higher than a decade low (Figure 3). TOT has likely reached a bottom, but its recovery remains slow and modest, as the economy<sup>’</sup>s key tech export prices are still in the early stages of their upturn.

Figure 3: Korea trade balance and terms of trade
![](images/d79330ef38f9f371599f018e6bcf16ed210f3328504fe9eb98c8b38927abe957.jpg)
Source: BoK and J.P. Morgan

Excluding the FX effects, tech export prices in contractual currency terms rose 0.7% in December, rising for four consecutive months by 1.6% on average. Prices of semiconductors rose 2.3% in December on top of gains of 11.9% in October and 2.9% in November. Tech prices have steadily increased since September, and their sequential inflation strengthened to 23.1% 3m/3m, saar, in December, for the third month in positive territory after two years of decline, which may be an early signal of their long-awaited cyclical upturn.

Meanwhile, ex-tech-and-oil export prices rose more modestly by 0.2%m/m and 1.7%3m/3m, saar, in December. In the details, export prices generally marked only slight increases during the month, across metal (0.7%m/m) and chemical

(0.1%) products, general machinery (0.1%) and transport equipment (0.0%).

## ASEAN

• Philippines growth momentum continues to ease going into 2024

BSP to stay on hold in 1H24 on CPI volatility; we pencil in 50bps of rate cuts in 2H24 with bias for more

Despite wider November trade deficit, CA narrowing in 2024 is still in the cards

Growth momentum in the Philippines continues to soften going into the new year as elevated financing costs and inflation weigh on domestic demand. We continue to expect a bumpy disinflation path, with dynamics surrounding rice prices a major driver. BSP will likely maintain a restrictive policy stance in 1H24 before delivering 50bps of rate cuts in 2H24, though the bias is skewed to more easing. Despite a weak November trade print, the CA improvement narrative in 2024 is still intact, premised on weaker domestic demand and potentially softer global commodity prices.

## New year, old story

Recent high-frequency data continue to point to a softening in growth momentum heading into 2024. Exports posted a decent monthly gain of 2.9%m/m, sa in November, but the underlying trend deteriorated to -8.3%3m/3m, saar from a peak of 36.8%3m/3m, saar in July. This was driven largely by the normalization of semiconductor shipments, which have seen an unusual surge through the most part of 2023. Given its dominance (60% of exports), the electronics sector is pivotal to the Philippines<sup>’</sup>s trade. In this regard, we adopt a cautious stance given that the ongoing regional tech cycle lift has been mostly confined to upstream producers in North Asia, and that US capex is expected to make only a modest recovery, with downside risk (Figure 1).

Figure 1: US durable equipment investment vs. Philippines exports
![](images/cf423cbb84569a972b11adab21361853add1761454e33987a252739c757eced6.jpg)

Another under-appreciated headwind is the lagged impact of BSP<sup>’</sup>s monetary tightening. Our analysis shows that historically, the policy transmission to monetary conditions has been reasonably efficient (Figure 2). With the real policy rate rising to multi-year highs, we are already seeing credit demand

weakening significantly, weighing on loans formation, especially in interest-sensitive/capex-intensive sectors such as construction and real estate. The implication is that even if scheduled public capex picks up (2024F: 11%), private investment activity could go in the opposite direction.

Household consumption is also facing cross currents. While the unemployment rate has declined to a post-pandemic low of 4.2%, job creation has slowed considerably (Figure 3), indicating that the best of labor market gains are behind us. Consumer confidence has also soured during the past holiday season, perhaps reflecting lingering concerns on inflation and could signal weaker private consumption (Figure 4).

Given these headwinds, we retain our below-consensus GDP estimate of 4.2% in 2024 (2023F: 5.1%).

Figure 2: Philippines real policy rate vs. credit demand vs. loans
![](images/d619d3e5a92b2a4e680096ceffcd8afc48984aee9a2592f0245a4edf0c1fb732.jpg)

Figure 3: Philippines active job postings
![](images/d4c9857a111bb86e5736e3c37b9c1628ba9db6d12fc2ee071acbf80425ebad06.jpg)
Source: Revelio Labs, J.P. Morgan

Figure 4: Consumer expectataions survey vs. private consumption Diffusion index 4:
![](images/b4d63facbfaf6f73166f74f67bd1679a333bba62e4cebd9706c2e35ba06e6ec9.jpg)
Source: CEIC, J.P. Morgan

## Rice against the rest

Headline inflation moderated to 3.9%oya in December, falling to the 2%-4% target range for the first time since March 2022. Per the last few prints, lower global energy prices have filtered through into domestic pump prices and utility prices, with additional positive knock-on effects on core inflation. Except rice, food price pressures have also dissipated, reflecting softer global agricultural prices and less-than-severe El Nino disruptions (Figure 5).

With this broad-based disinflation across energy, food, and core components, the question is how much upside risk could rice prices exert on overall CPI? Assuming the trajectories of previous rice inflation episodes in 2014 and 2018 (Figure 6), we project that rice prices will contribute 0.8%pt. to overall CPI in 2024 (2010-19 average: 0.2%pt.), with the bulk of the pressures concentrated in 1H24.

Figure 5: Philippines food inflation %pt. contribution to healdine %oya
![](images/3d4f996d3caeadf899b9c4438d61f039e92952ae39001baa5b8d12111184c21a.jpg)

Figure 6: Philippines rice prices
![](images/04cc647132a914e4829659b1cdf7097409d862cdd8a36ce9e4f78c503337f3fb.jpg)

We continue to expect the disinflation path (2023: 6.0% vs. 2024F: 3.9%) to be bumpy, with a sustained downtrend towards target range achieved starting in 3Q24. This means that the BSP will likely maintain a restrictive policy stance in 1H24 and deliver 50bps of easing in 2H24, in tandem with the anticipated Fed rate cut cycle. In our view, the bias is for more easing in 2H24, given a high ex-ante real rate and a potential dovish shift in the Monetary Board composition (see link).

## CA recovery still in the cards

The trade deficit widened from US\$4.4 billion in October to US\$4.7 billion in November, driven by a higher-than-expected increase in raw materials (3.6%pt. contribution to headline monthly increase of 3.7%m/m, sa) and consumer goods (1.2%pt.) imports.

Despite the negative surprise, we think that the current account (CA) improvement narrative in 2024 is intact. Philippines is one of the largest commodity importers in Asia and is thus highly sensitive to terms-of-trade (Figure 7). To the extent that global commodity prices could soften further in a soft-landing scenario, we think that the Philippines is in a prime position to benefit from an external standpoint.

Figure 7: EM Asia commodity balance vs. CRB-goods balance correlation Correlation between CRB index and goods balance (2020-23)
![](images/7034115742d7600814dd5421357e4296bdd07d25c1d5ee9c3616442a27430bce.jpg)
Source: CEIC, J.P. Morgan estimates 1. SITC sections 0-4; 2020-23 average

As mentioned previously, persistent weakness in private capex against the backdrop of elevated funding costs, could also lead to lower raw materials and capital goods imports in 2024. The key upside risk is a strong upturn in public infrastructure investments, but we think that there are two mitigating factors. One, compared to the Duterte administration, major infrastructure projects under President Marcos are more dependent on the PPP model, which is subject to global economic conditions. And two, our channel checks show that despite remarkable efforts to expedite approval times at the national level, there are still significant execution challenges at the local government unit (LGU) level.

Following the November trade print, we now expect the CA deficit to narrow from 3.5% of GDP in 2023 to 1.7% of GDP in 2024 (Table 1).
