## India

Trade deficit at a five-month low on healthy merchandise exports

• However, services exports witness a sharp decline

• CAD forecasted at 1.3% of GDP in FY24

• WPI inflation stays soft in December

The trade data reveals that merchandise exports are holding up even as imports have been subdued. However, trends in exports are a mixed bag, as service exports dropped sharply in December. We will be closely watching January service trade data to see if the weakness in December was one-off or a trend. Meanwhile, we expect widening of the Current Account Deficit (CAD) from 1% of GDP in Q323 to 1.6% of GDP in Q423, as oil imports normalize from weak levels. For the full year this would translate into a CAD of 1.3% of GDP in FY24. December WPI remained soft with a modest sequential increase as higher food prices were offset by subdued commodity prices.

## December trade deficit narrows to a fivemonth low

The trade deficit printed at a five-month low of \$19.8 bn in December, narrowing from \$20.6 bn in November. The easing in the trade deficit was led by exports: exports ex oil saw a 9-month high in USD levels translating into a sequential growth of 2.2% m/m, sa in December. This also helped offset firming (1.4% m/m, sa) in non-oil-non-gold (NONG) imports in December. More broadly, the trends in trade data reveal that merchandise export have held up well with last four months<sup>’</sup> non-oil exports averaging sequential growth of 1.1% (Figure 1). In contrast, imports have been soft with NONG sequential growth averaging -1.7% in last four months (Figure 2). Meanwhile, gold imports have begun to soften with gold imports printing \$3.1 bn in December vis-à-vis last four months<sup>’</sup> average of \$5.5 bn.

Figure 1: Exports ex-oil
![](images/6e46f58e7aab5801b9b64e190388a1d211aa44936ca3a0d81c092f62c0d658bb.jpg)

Figure 2: Non-oil non-gold imports
![](images/fd8b68338f3afc3878bbc3d30fa5b82e4375432d1d1e8cdb954427f8626dcb85.jpg)

## Service import and export growth falls

The services trade data in December was disappointing with gross service exports and imports both contracting by 11% m/ m, sa in December. In absolute terms, the declines are modest, however, December being a calendar year end, typically witnesses large sequential uptick in service exports and imports which did not play out this year. The weakness is also captured in % oya terms with gross service exports growth falling to -10.6% in December from 4.3% in November (Figure 3). The data for trade services for January would be keenly awaited to see if December<sup>’</sup>s weakness was an aberration or part of a trend. All in all, net service exports eased just marginally from \$14.6bn in December to \$14.4 in November (Figure 3).

Figure 3: Service export and imports % oya, 3mma
![](images/68b9fc16ae47eac0d5c10a949d1c5d523ecc604af44ca05f104e57fb9e75e759.jpg)

## CAD forecasted at 1.3% of GDP in FY24

While trends augur well for the trade deficit in the coming months, on a quarterly basis the merchandise trade deficit has widened to \$70 bn in 4Q23 (October-December) from \$61 bn. This has occurred as net oil imports rose sharply to \$26 bn in 4Q from exceptionally soft \$18 bn in 3Q23. This higher trade deficit would be partly offset by higher invisibles with net service exports in level terms higher in 4Q vis-à-vis 3Q23. All in all, we expect the current account deficit to widen to \$15 bn (1.6% of GDP) in 4Q23 from \$8 bn (1% of GDP) in 3Q23 (Figure 4). For the full year this would translate into CAD of 1.3% of GDP(\~\$45 bn) in FY24.

Figure 4: Current account deficit
![](images/e7f4a607b130d81b04a97ac737f8d4fccda147883e36afccf383217282d1078e.jpg)
Source: RBI, J.P. Morgan

## WPI remains soft in December

Wholesale Price Index (WPI) inflation printed at 0.7% oya in December inching up from 0.3% in November. On a sequential basis, WPI rose just 0.1% m/m sa in December, despite a pick up in food prices in December (1.2% m/m, sa), with a correction in commodity prices offsetting the impact. The core prices rose by 0.6% m/m, sa in December after a 2.1% fall in November. On % oya basis, core inflation remains weak at -0.5% oya reflecting subdued commodity prices (Figure 5). Going forward, WPI inflation, absent a commodity prices shock, is expected to remain soft in the coming months.

Figure 5: Wholesale price index (WPI)
![](images/47fa8a94dedec2cd381eb6c73bee43c0c49814a36296f839eac7a7409cfda8d3.jpg)
Source: MoSPI

## Data releases and forecasts

No data releases.
